"Religion says: --- 'Believe and you will understand.' Science comes to say to you: --- 'Understand you will believe.'
"At that moment the whole of science will change front; the spirit, so long dethroned and forgottwill take its ancient place; it will be demonstrated that the old traditions are all true, that tewoe of paganism is only a system of corrupted and misplaced truths, that it is sufficient to clens tem so to say, and to put them back again in their place, to see them shine with all their ray. n awor, all ideas will change, and since on all sides a multitude of the elect cry in concert,'Com, Lod, cme!' why should you blame the men who throw themselves forward into that majestic futre, ad prie theselves on having foreseen it?"

(J. De Maistre, "Soirees de St. Petersbourg.")



TRANSLATOR'S NOTE

IN the biographical and critical essay which Mr. Waite prefixes to his "Mysteries of Magic" he sa"A word must be added of the method of this digest, which claims to be something more than translto nd has been infinitely more laborious. I believe it to be in all respects faithful, and whereitha ben necessary or possible for it to be literal, there also it is invariably literal."
We agree that it is either more or less than translation, and the following examples selected at rd in the course of half-an-hour will enable the reader to judge whether Mr. Waite is acquainted ihether French or English:
"Gentilhomme" --- "Gentleman."
"The nameless vice which was reproached "against" the Templars."
"Certaines circonstances ridicules et un proces en escroquerie" --- "Certain ridiculous processes a swindling lawsuit."
"Se mele de dogmatiser" --- "Meddles with dogmatism."
"La vie pour lui suffisait a l'expiation des plus grands crimes, puis qu'elle etait la consequencun arret de mort" --- "According to him life was sufficient for the greatest crimes, since "these eethe result of a death sentence."
"Vos meilleurs amis ont du concevoir des inquietudes" --- "Your best friends have been reasonablyious." (The mistranslation here turns the speech into an insult.) {v}
"Sacro-sainte" --- "Sacred and saintly."
"Auriculaire" --- "Index."
"N'avez vous pas obtenu tout ce que vous demandiez, et plus que vous ne demandiez, car vous ne m'z pas parle d'argent?" --- "Have you not had all and more than you wanted, and there has been no usin of remuneration?" (This mistranslation makes nonsense of the whole passage.)
"Eliphas n'etait pas a la question" --- "Eliphas was not under cross- examination."
"Mauvais plaisant" --- "Vicious jester."
"Si vous n'aviez pas ... vous deviendriez" --- "If you have not ... you may become." (This mistration turns a compliment into an insult.)
"An awful and ineffaceable tableaux."
"Peripeties" --- "Circumstances."
"Il avait fait partie du clerge de Saint Germain l'Auxerrois" --- "He was of the Society of St. Gin l'Auxerrois."
"Bruit de tempete" --- "Stormy sound."
We are obliged to mention this matter, as Mr. Waite (by persistent self- assertion) has obtained reputation of being trustworthy as an editor. On the contrary, he not only mutilates and distortshsathors, but, as demonstrated above, he is totally incapable of understanding their simplest phrse ad ven their commonest words. {vi}



INTRODUCTION

THIS volume represents the high-water mark of the thought of Eliphas Levi. It may be regarded astten by him as his Thesis for the Grade of Exempt Adept, just as his "Ritual and Dogma" was his Tei or the grade of a Major Adept. He is, in fact, no longer talking of things as if their sense asfiedand universal. He is beginning to see something of the contradiction inherent in the natur ofthigs,or at any rate, he constantly illustrates the fact that the planes are to be kept separae fo pratica purposes, although in the final analysis they turn out to be one. This, and the extrordinrily ubtleand delicate irony of which Eliphas Levi is one of the greatest masters that has eer livd, hav baffld the pedantry and stupidity of such commentators as Waite. English has hardlya word o expres the mntal condition of such unfortunates. "Dummheit," in its strongest German sese, is aout the earest ting to it. It is as if a geographer should criticize "Gulliver's Travels from hisown partiular stanpoint.
When Levi says that all that he asserts as an initiate is subordinate to his humble submissivenes a Christian, and then not only remarks that the Bible and the Qur'an are different translations ftesame book, but treats the Incarnation as an allegory, it is evident that a good deal of submisio wllbe required. When he agrees with St. Augustine that a thing is not just because God wills t, ut od ills it because it is just, he sees perfectly well that he is reducing God to a poetic iage efleted rom his own moral {vii} ideal of justice, and no amount of alleged orthodoxy can weig agaist tht staement. His very defence of the Catholic Hierarchy is a masterpiece of that peculir formof concious ophistry which justifies itself by reducing its conclusion to zero. One must bgin wit "one,"and tha "one" has no particular qualities. Therefore, so long as you have an authoity proprly centalized i does not really matter what that authority is. In the Pope we have suchan authorty ready ade, and t is the gravest tactical blunder to endeavour to set up an authority pposed to im. Succes in doingso means war, and failure anarchy. This, however, did not prevent evi from ceemonially csting a papl crown to the ground and crying "Death to tyranny and superstiton!" in the osom of a cetain secret reopagus of which he was the most famous member.
When a man becomes a magician he looks about him for a magical weapon; and, being probably endoweth that human frailty called laziness, he hopes to find a weapon ready made. Thus we find the Chita Magus who imposed his power upon the world taking the existing worships and making a single sstm omining all their merits. There is no single feature in Christianity which has not been take boilyfro the worship of Isis, or of Mithras, or of Bacchus, or of Adonis, or of Osiris. In moden ties aain e find Frater Iehi Aour trying to handle Buddhism. Others again have attempted to us Freeasonr. Thre have been even exceptionally foolish magicians who have tried to use a sword log sinc ruste.
Wagner illustrates this point very clearly in "Siegfried." The Great Sword Nothung has been brokand it is the {viii} only weapon that can destroy the gods. The dwarf Mime tries uselessly to medi. When Siegfried comes he makes no such error. He melts its fragments and forges a new sword. Inspteof the intense labour which this costs, it is the best plan to adopt.
Levi completely failed to capture Catholicism; and his hope of using Imperialism, his endeavour trsuade the Emperor that he was the chosen instrument of the Almighty, a belief which would have ealdhim to play Maximus to little Napoleon's Julian, was shattered once for all at Sedan.
It is necessary for the reader to gain this clear conception of Levi's inmost mind, if he is to rcile the "contradictions" which leave Waite petulant and bewildered. It is the sad privilege of h iher order of mind to be able to see both sides of every question, and to appreciate the fact tatboh re equally tenable. Such contradictions can, of course, only be reconciled on a higher plae, nd hismethod of harmonizing contradictions is, therefore, the best key to the higher planes.
It seems unnecessary to add anything to these few remarks. This is the only difficulty in the whbook, though in one or two passages Levi's extraordinarily keen sense of humour leads him to indug na little harmless bombast. We may instance his remarks on the "Grimoire" of Honorius.
We have said that this is the masterpiece of Levi. He reaches an exaltation of both thought and uage which is equal to that of any other writer known to us. Once it is understood that it is pueyathesis for the Grade of Exempt Adept, the reader should have no further difficulty. --- A. C. ix



PREFACE

On the brink of mystery, the spirit of man is seized with giddiness. Mystery is the abyss which clessly attracts our unquiet curiosity by the terror of its depth.
The greatest mystery of the infinite is the existence of Him for whom alone all is without myster Comprehending the infinite which is essentially incomprehensible, He is Himself that infinite andenally unfathomable mystery; that is to say, that He is, in all seeming, that supreme absurdity i hchTertullian believed.
Necessarily absurd, since reason must renounce for ever the project of attaining to Him; necessarcredible, since science and reason, far from demonstrating that He does not exist, are dragged bytecariot of fatality to believe that He does exist, and to adore Him themselves with closed eyes. Wh? -- Because this Absurd is the infinite source of reason. The light springs eternally from he teral hadows. Science, that Babel Tower of the spirit, may twist and coil its spirals ever asendig asit wll; it may make the earth tremble, it will never touch the sky.
God is He whom we shall eternally learn to know better, and, consequently, He whom we shall neverw entirely.
The realm of mystery is, then, a field open to the conquests of the intelligence. March there asdly as you will, never will you diminish its extent; you will only alter {xi} its horizons. To ko l is an impossible dream; but woe unto him who dares not to learn all, and who does not know tht,inorer to know anything, one must learn eternally!
They say that in order to learn anything well, one must forget it several times. The world has fwed this method. Everything which is to-day debateable had been solved by the ancients. Before u nals began, their solutions, written in hieroglyphs, had already no longer any meaning for us. A anha rediscovered their key; he has opened the cemeteries of ancient science, and he gives to hs cntuy awhole world of forgotten theorems, of syntheses as simple and sublime as nature, radiatig alays rom nity, and multiplying themselves like numbers with proportions so exact, that the knon demnstraes an reveals the unknown. To understand this science, is to see God. The author of tis boo, as h finises his work, will think that he has demonstrated it.
Then, when you have seen God, the hierophant will say to you: --- "Turn round!" and, in the shadoich you throw in the presence of this sun of intelligences, there will appear to you the devil, ta lck phantom which you see when your gaze is not fixed upon God, and when you think that your shdo fll the sky, --- for the vapours of the earth, the higher they go, seem to magnify it more andmor.
To harmonize in the category of religion science with revelation and reason with faith, to demonse in philosophy the absolute principles which reconcile all the antinomies, and finally to revealteuiversal equilibrium of natural forces, is the triple object of this work, which will consequenlybediided into three parts. {xii}
We shall exhibit true religion with such characters, that no one, believer or unbeliever, can fai recognize it; that will be the absolute in religion. We shall establish in philosophy the immutbecaracters of that Truth, which is in science, "reality;" in judgment, "reason;" and in ethics, jutie. Finally, we shall acquaint you with the laws of Nature, whose equilibrium is stability, ad w shll how how vain are the phantasies of our imagination before the fertile realities of movemnt ad oflife We shall also invite the great poets of the future to create once more the divine cmedy,no loger acording to the dreams of man, but according to the mathematics of God.
Mysteries of other worlds, hidden forces, strange revelations, mysterious illnesses, exceptional lties, spirits, apparitions, magical paradoxes, hermetic arcana, we shall say all, and we shall epanall. Who has given us this power? We do not fear to reveal it to our readers.
There exists an occult and sacred alphabet which the Hebrews attribute to Enoch, the Egyptians toth or to Hermes Trismegistus, the Greeks to Cadmus and to Palamedes. This alphabet was known to h olowers of Pythagoras, and is composed of absolute ideas attached to signs and numbers; by its omintins, it realizes the mathematics of thought. Solomon represented this alphabet by seventy-tw naes,writen upon thirty-six talismans. Eastern initiates still call these the "little keys" or laviles f Soomon. These keys are described, and their use explained, in a book the source of whoe traitionl doga is the patriarch Abraham. This book is called the Sepher Yetzirah; with the aidof theSepherYetzirh one can penetrate the {xiii} hidden sense of the Zohar, the great dogmatic tratise o the Qaalah ofthe Hebrews. The Clavicles of Solomon, forgotten in the course of time, andsupposedlost, hae been rdiscovered by ourselves; without trouble we have opened all the doors of hose old anctuarie where abolute truth seemed to sleep, --- always young, and always beautiful, lke that prncess of te childishlegend, who, during a century of slumber, awaits the bridegroom whoe mission i is to awakn her.
After our book, there will still be mysteries, but higher and farther in the infinite depths. Thublication is a light or a folly, a mystification or a monument. Read, reflect, and judge.

PART I

RELIGIOUS MYSTERIES

PROBLEMS FOR SOLUTION

I. --- To demonstrate in a certain and absolute manner the existence of God, and to give an idea im which will satisfy all minds.
II. --- To establish the existence of a true religion in such a way as to render it incontestable III. --- To indicate the bearing and the "raison d'etre" of all the mysteries of the one true andnvrsal religion.
IV. --- To turn the objections of philosophy into arguments favourable to true religion.
V. --- To draw the boundary between religion and superstition, and to give the reason of miracles prodigies.

PRELIMINARY CONSIDERATIONS

WHEN Count Joseph de Maistre, that grand and passionate lover of Logic, said despairingly, "The w is without religion," he resembled those people who say rashly "There is no God."
The world, in truth, is without the religion of Count Joseph de Maistre, as it is probable that sa God as the majority of atheists conceive does not exist.
Religion is an idea based upon one constant and universal {1} fact; man is a religious animal. Tord "religion" has then a necessary and absolute sense. Nature herself sanctifies the idea whichti ord represents, and exalts it to the height of a principle.
The need of believing is closely linked with the need of loving; for that reason our souls need cnion in the same hopes and in the same love. Isolated beliefs are only doubts: it is the bond ofmta confidence which, by creating faith, composes religion.
Faith does not invent itself, does not impose itself, does not establish itself by any political ement; like life, it manifests itself with a sort of fatality. The same power which directs the hnmna of nature, extends and limits the supernatural domain of faith, despite all human foresight nedos not imagine revelations; one undergoes then, and one believes in them. In vain does the pirt potet against the obscurities of dogma; it is subjugated by the attraction of these very obsurites, nd oten the least docile of reasoners would blush to accept the title of "irreligious man"
Religion holds a greater place among the realities of life than those who do without religion ---pretend to do without it --- affect to believe. All ideas that raise man above the animal --- moa oe, devotion, honour --- are sentiments essentially religious. The cult of the fatherland and f hefaily, fidelity to an oath and to memory, are things which humanity will never abjure withoutdegadig iself utterly, and which could never exist without the belief in something greater than mrtallife wit all its vicissitudes, its ignorance and its misery.
If annihilation were the result of all our aspirations to {2} those sublime things which we feel e eternal, our only duties would be the enjoyment of the present, forgetfulness of the past, and aeesness about the future, and it would be rigorously true to say, as a celebrated sophist once sid tatthe man who thinks is a degraded animal.
Moreover, of all human passions, religious passion is the most powerful and the most lively. It rates itself, whether by affirmation or negation, with an equal fanaticism, some obstinately affimn he god that they have made in their own image, the others denying God with rashness, as if the hd ee able to understand and to lay waste by a single thought all that world of infinity which prtans o Hs great name.
Philosophers have not sufficiently considered the physiological fact of religion in humanity, fortruth religion exists apart from all dogmatic discussion. It is a faculty of the human soul justa uh as intelligence and love. While man exists, so will religion. Considered in this light, itisnohig but the need of an infinite idealism, a need which justifies every aspiration for progres, wichinsires every devotion, which alone prevents virtue and honour from being mere words, servig toexplit te vanity of the weak and the foolish to the profit of the strong and the clever.
It is to this innate need of belief that one might justly give the name of natural religion; and which tends to clip the wings of these beliefs is, on the religious plane, in opposition to natur. h essence of the object of religion is mystery, since faith begins with the unknown, abandoningth rstto the investigations of science. Doubt is, moreover, the mortal enemy of faith; faith fees tat he ntervention of {3} the divine being is necessary to fill the abyss which separates the fnitefromthe nfinite, and it affirms this intervention with all the warmth of its heart, with all he doilityof it intelligence. If separated from this act of faith, the need of religion finds nosatisfction,and tuns to scepticism and to despair. But in order that the act of faith should notbe an at of foly, reaon wishes it to be directed and ruled. By what? By science? We have seen hat sciece can d nothinghere. By the civil authority? It is absurd. Are our prayers to be supeintended y policemn?
There remains, then, moral authority, which alone is able to constitute dogma and establish the dpline of worship, in concert this time with the civil authority, but not in obedience to its ordes I is necessary, in a word, that faith should give to the religious need a real satisfaction, -- asaisaction entire, permanent and indubitable. To obtain that, it is necessary to have the absouteandinvriable affirmation of a dogma preserved by an authorized hierarchy. It is necessary to ave n eficacous cult, giving, with an absolute faith, a substantial realization of the symbols ofbelie.
Religion thus understood being the only one which can satisfy the natural need of religion, it mue the only really natural religion. We arrive, without help from others, at this double definitin ht true natural religion is revealed religion. The true revealed religion is the hierarchical ndtrdiional religion, which affirms itself absolutely, above human discussion, by communion in fath,hop, ad charity.
Representing the moral authority, and realizing it by the efficacy of its ministry, the priesthoo as holy and infallible as humanity is subject to vice and to error. The priest, {4} "qua" pries,i lways the representative of God. Of little account are the faults or even the crimes of man. Whn leander VI consecrated his bishops, it was not the poisoner who laid his hands upon them, it as he ope Pope Alexander VI never corrupted or falsified the dogmas which condemned him, or the acraentswhic in his hands saved others, and did not justify him. At all times and in all places here ave ben lirs and criminals, but in the hierarchical and divinely authorized Church there hav neverbeen, nd thee will never be, either bad popes or bad priests. "Bad" and "priest" form an oymoron. We hve mentoned Alexander VI, and we think that this name will be sufficient without other memoes as jutly execated as his being brought up against us. Great criminals have been able to dishoor themseles doublybecause of the sacred character with which they were invested, but they had no te power todishonour hat character, which remains always radiant and splendid above fallen humanty.<A dog has ix legs. Dfinition. It is no answer to this to show that all dogs have four. --- .M.>
We have said that there is no religion without mysteries; let us add that there are no mysteries out symbols. The symbol, being the formula or the expression of the mystery, only expresses its nnw depth by paradoxical images borrowed from the known. The symbolic form, having for its objec t carcterize what is above scientific reason, should necessarily find itself without that reason hece he elebrated and perfectly just remark of a Father of the Church: "I believe because it is bsur. Cedo uia absurdum."
If science were to affirm what it did not know, it would {5} destroy itself. Science will then n be able to perform the work of faith, any more than faith can decide in a matter of science. Anafration of faith with which science is rash enough to meddle can then be nothing but an absurdit fr t,just as a scientific statement, if given us as an article of faith, would be an absurdity o th reigius plane. To know and to believe are two terms which can never be confounded.
It would be equally impossible to oppose the one to the other. It is impossible, in fact, to bel the contrary of what one knows without ceasing, for that very reason, to know it; and it is equal mossible to achieve a knowledge contrary to what one believes without ceasing immediately to beiee. To deny or even to contest the decisions of faith in the name of science is to prove that one undtans neither science nor faith: in fine, the mystery of a God of three persons is not a problem o ahemaics; the incarnation of the Word is not a phenomenon in obstetrics; the scheme of redemptio sans apat from the criticism of the historian. Science is absolutely powerless to decide whethe weareright r wrong in believing or disbelieving dogma; it can only observe the results of belief andif fith eviently improves men, if, moreover, faith is in itself, considered as a physiologica fact evidntly a ncessity and a force, science will certainly be obliged to admit it, and take th wise art ofalways rekoning with it.
Let us now dare to affirm that there exists an immense fact equally appreciable both by faith andence; a fact which makes God visible (in a sense) upon earth; a fact incontestable and of universlbaing; this fact is the manifestation in the world, beginning from the epoch when the {6} Christanreeltion was made, of a spirit unknown to the ancients, of a spirit evidently divine, more posiivetha scence in its works, in its aspirations, more magnificently ideal than the highest poetry,a sprit or wich it was necessary to create a new name, a name altogether unheard<<Who, however, hd theword aid aide against the time when Paul should give it a meaning. --- O.M.>> in the sanctuaies ofantiquty. Tis name was created, and we shall demonstrate that this name, this word, is, inreligio, as muh for sience as for faith, the expression of the absolute. The word is CHARITY, an the spiit of whch we spak is the "spirit of charity."
Before charity, faith prostrates itself, and conquered science bows. There is here evidently someg greater than humanity; charity proves by its works that it is not a dream. It is stronger thanalte passions; it triumphs over suffering and over death; it makes God understood by every heart,an sem already to fill eternity by the begun realization of its legitimate hopes.
Before charity alive and in action who is the Proudhon who dares blaspheme? Who is the Voltaire dares laugh?
Pile one upon the other the sophisms of Diderot, the critical arguments of Strauss, the "Ruins" olney, so well named, for this man could make nothing but "ruins," the blasphemies of the revolutinwoe voice was extinguished once in blood, and once again in the silence of contempt; join to it lltht he future may hold for us of monstrosities and of vain dreams; then will there come the humles an th simplest of all sisters of charity, --- the world will leave there all its follies, andall ts cimes and all its dreams, to bow before this sublime reality. {7}
Charity! word divine, sole word which makes God understood, word which contains a universal reveln! "Spirit" of "charity," alliance of two words, which are a complete solution and a complete prms! To what question, in fine, do these two words not find an answer?
What is God for us, if not the spirit of charity? What is orthodoxy? Is it not the spirit of cha which refuses to discuss faith lest it should trouble the confidence of simple souls, and distur h eace of universal communion?<<Sublime houmour of sophistry! Levi asserts, "Any lie will serve povde every one acquiesces in it," and reprehends Christianity for disturbing the peace of Pagansm. "O," ndicates that Christianity is but syncretic-eclectic Paganism, and defends it on this grund.--- .M.> And the universal church, is it any other thing than a communion in the spirit of carity It s by he spirit of charity that the church is infallible. It is the spirit of charity wich isthe diine vitue of the priesthood.
Duty of man, guarantee of his rights, proof of his immortality, eternity of happiness commencing him upon the earth, glorious aim given to his existence, goal and path of all his struggles, perfcinof his individual, civil and religious morality, the spirit of charity understands all, and isabe o ope all, undertake all, and accomplish all.
It is by the spirit of charity that Jesus expiring on the cross gave a son to His mother in the pn of St. John, and, triumphing over the anguish of the most frightful torture, gave a cry of delieac and of salvation, saying, "Father, into Thy hands I commend my spirit!"
It is by charity that twelve Galilean artisans conquered the world; they loved truth more than liand they went without followers to speak it to peoples and to kings; tested by torture, {8} they eefund faithful. They showed to the multitude a living immortality in their death, and they wateedth erth with a blood whose heat could not be extinguished, because they were burning with the adous o chrity.
It is by charity that the Apostles built up their Creed. They said that to believe together was h more than to doubt separately; they constituted the hierarchy on the basis of obedience --- renee o noble and so great by the spirit of charity, that to serve in this manner is to reign; they orulte the faith of all and the hope of all, and they put this Creed in the keeping of the charit ofall We to the egoist who appropriates to himself a single word of this inheritance of the Wor; heis adeicde, who wishes to dismember the body of the Lord.
This creed is the holy ark of charity; whoso touches it is stricken by eternal death, for charityhdraws itself from him. It is the sacred inheritance of our children, it is the price of the blodo ur fathers!
It is by charity that the martyrs took consolation in the prisons of the Caesars, and won over toir belief even their warders and their executioners.
It is in the name of charity that St. Martin of Tours protested against the torture of the Priscins,<<The Priscillianist heresy was disturbing the Church, especially in Spain. The Emperor Maxims paniard, was inclined to put it down with a strong hand and confiscate the heretics' property. Te alic clergy hounded him on, and the Councils of Bordeaux and Saragossa encouraged him. Two Sanih piess, "Ithacus" and "Idacus," clamoured for the heretics' punishment by the secular arm. Bt St Marin o Tours, stalwart champion of orthodoxy as he was, resisted, and in 385 he went to Trees topleadfor te persecuted Priscillianists. He prevailed. So long as Martin stayed at court theIthaca partywas foled. When he left they had the upper hand again, and Maximus gave the suppresson of te heretcs intothe hands of the unrelenting Evodious. Priscillian was killed. Exile and dath werethe fateof his fllowers. Heresy blazed the stronger, and a worse persecution was threateed. ThenSt. Marti left hiscell at Marmontier, and set out a second time to Treves. News of the ld man comng along te road on is ass reached his enemies. They met him at the gate and refused hm entrance. "But," sai Martin, "Icome with the peace of Jesus Christ." And such was the power ofthis presenc that they culd not clos the city gates against him. But the palace doors were close. Martin refsed to see th Ithacans or o receive the Communion with them, and their fury at this s eloquent tesimony of theirsense of his pwer. They appealed to Maximus, who delivered over Martn bound to them But in the niht Maximus sentfor Martin, argued, coaxed, persuaded him to compromse. The schism ould be great, h persisted, if Mrtin continued to exasperate the Ithacans. Marti said he had nothng to do with perecutors. In wrat the Emperor let him go, and gave orders to th Tribunes to depar to Spain and carr out a rigorous Inuisition. Then Martin returned to Maximus nd bargained. Let his order be revoke, and he would receve Communion with the Ithacans next day t the election of th new Archbishop. Th order was revoked, nd Martin kept his word. But when heknew the cause of Humnity safe, he departe, and on his way backto Tours experienced a great agony Why had he had dealigs with the Ithacans? In a lonely place he pndered sadly. An angel spoke t him. "Martin, you do rght to be sad, but it ws the only way." Neveragain did he go to any councl. He was wont to say wth tears that if he had aved the heretics he himelf had lost power over mn and over demons.
They have outraged the meaning of the episode who explain Martin's protest as merely against the ender of the Church to Secular Power. It was "lese-humanite" of which he held the Ithacans guilt. t. Martin of Tours was often called Martin the Thaumaturgist. He was noted for his power over amas.> and separated {9} himself from the communion of the tyrant who wished to impose faith by th wor.
It is by charity that so great a crowd of saints have forced the world to accept them as expiatior the crimes committed in the name of religion itself, and the scandals of the profaned sanctuary
It is by charity that St. Vincent de Paul and Fenelon compelled the admiration of even the most ius centuries, and quelled in advance the laughter of the children of Voltaire before the imposingdgiy of their virtues. 10}
It is by charity, finally, that the folly of the cross has become the wisdom of the nations, becaevery noble heart has understood that it is greater to believe with those who love, and who devot hmelves, than to doubt with the egotists and with the slaves of pleasure. {11}



FIRST ARTICLE

SOLUTION OF THE FIRST PROBLEM

THE TRUE GOD

GOD can only be defined by faith; science can neither deny nor affirm that He exists.
God is the absolute object of human faith. In the infinite, He is the supreme and creative intelnce of order. In the world, He is the spirit of charity.
Is the Universal Being a fatal machine which eternally grinds down intelligences by chance, or a idential intelligence which directs forces in order to ameliorate minds?
The first hypothesis is repugnant to reason; it is pessimistic and immoral.
Science and reason ought then to accept the second.
Yes, Proudhon, God is an hypothesis, but an hypothesis so necessary, that without it, all theoremcome absurd or doubtful.
For initiates of the Qabalah, God is the absolute unity which creates and animates numbers.
The unity of the human intelligence demonstrates the unity of God.
The key of numbers is that of creeds, because signs are {12} analogical figures of the harmony whproceeds from numbers.
Mathematics could never demonstrate blind fatality, because they are the expression of the exacti which is the character of the highest reason.
Unity demonstrates the analogy of contraries; it is the foundation, the equilibrium, and the end umbers. The act of faith starts from unity, and returns to unity.

We shall now sketch out an explanation of the Bible by the aid of numbers, for the Bible is the bof the images of God.
We shall ask numbers to give us the reason of the dogmas of eternal religion; numbers will alwaysly by reuniting themselves in the synthesis of unity.
The following pages are simply outlines of qabalistic hypotheses; they stand apart from faith, an indicate them only as curiosities of research. It is no part of our task to make innovations indga and what we assert in our character as an initiate is entirely subordinate to our submission n urchracter as a Christian.<<This passage is typical of the sublime irony of Levi, and the key t th whle f his paradoxes. --- TRANS.>>


SKETCH OF THE PROPHETIC THEOLOGY

OF NUMBERS

I

UNITY

UNITY is the principle and the synthesis of numbers; it is the idea of God and of man; it is the ance of reason and of faith.
Faith cannot be opposed to reason; it is made necessary by love, it is identical with hope. To lis to believe and hope; and this triple outburst of the soul is called virtue, because, in order omk it, courage is necessary. But would there be any courage in that, if doubt were not possible ow t be able to doubt, is to doubt. Doubt is the force {14} which balances faith, and it consttuts te wole merit of faith.
Nature herself induces us to believe; but the formulae of faith are social expressions of the tenies of faith at a given epoch. It is that which proves the Church to be infallible, evidentiallyadi fact.
God is necessarily the most unknown of all beings because He is only defined by negative experienHe is all that we are not, He is the infinite opposed to the finite by hypothesis.
Faith, and consequently hope and love, are so free that man, far from being able to impose them ohers, does not even impose them on himself.
"These," says religion, "are graces." Now, is it conceivable that grace should be subject to demor exaction; that is to say, could any one wish to force men to a thing which comes freely and wihu rice from heaven? One must not do more than desire it for them.
To reason concerning faith is to think irrationally, since the object of faith is outside the unie of reason. If one asks me: --- "Is there a God?" I reply, "I believe it." "But are you sure o t"--- "If I were sure of it, I should not believe it, I should know it."
The formulation of faith is to agree upon the terms of the common hypothesis.
Faith begins where science ends. To enlarge the scope of science is apparently to diminish that aith; but in reality, it is to enlarge it in equal proportion, for it is to amplify its base.
One can only define the unknown by its supposed and supposable relations with the known. {15}
Analogy was the sole dogma of the ancient magi. This dogma may indeed be called "mediator," for s half scientific, half hypothetical; half reason, and half poetry. This dogma has been, and wil las be, the father of all others.
What is the Man-God? He who realizes, in the most human life, the most divine ideal.
Faith is a divination of intelligence and of love, when these are directed by the pointings of na and of reason.
It is then of the essence of the things of faith to be inaccessible to science, doubtful for philhy, and undefined for certainty.
Faith is an hypothetical realization and a conventional determination of the last aims of hope. s the attachment to the visible sign of the things which one does not see.
"Faith is the substance of things hoped for, the evidence of things not seen."
To affirm without folly that God is or that He is not, one must begin with a reasonable or unreasle definition of God. Now, this definition, in order to be reasonable, must be hypothetical, anaoia, and the negation of the known finite. It is possible to deny a particular God, but the absout Gd an no more be denied than He can be proved; He is a reasonable supposition in whom one belives
"Blessed are the pure in heart, for they shall see God," said the Master; to see with the heart i believe; and if this faith is attached to the true good, it can never be deceived, provided thati os not seek to define too much in accordance with the dangerous inductions which spring from pesoaligorance. Our judgments in questions of faith apply to {16} ourselves; it will be done to usas e hve elieved; that is to say, we create ourselves in the image of our ideal.
"Those who make their gods become like unto them," says the psalmist, "and all they that put theiust in them."
The divine ideal of the ancient world made the civilization which came to an end, and one must nospair of seeing the god of our barbarous fathers become the devil of our more enlightened childre. n makes devils with cast-off gods,<<Christianity has fallen, and so Christ has already become te deil to such thinkers as Nietzsche and Crowley. --- O.M.>> and Satan is only so incoherent and o frmlss ecause he is made up of all the rags of ancient theogonies. He is the sphinx without a ecre, th ridle without an answer, the mystery without truth, the absolute without reality and witout lght.
Man is the son of God because God, manifested, realized, and incarnated upon earth, called Himsele Son of man.
It is after having made God in the image of His intelligence and of His love, that humanity has ustood the sublime Word who said "Let there be light!"
Man is the form of the divine thought, and God is the idealized synthesis of human thought.
Thus the Word of God reveals man, and the Word of man reveals God.
Man is the God of the world, and God is the man of heaven.
Before saying "God wills," man has willed.
In order to understand and honour Almighty God, man must first be free.
Had he obeyed and abstained from the fruit of the tree of knowledge through fear, man would have innocent and {17} stupid as the lamb, sceptical and rebellious as the angel of light. He himsel u he umbilical cord of his simplicity, and, falling free upon the earth, dragged God with him inhi fll
And therefore, from this sublime fall, he rises again glorious, with the great convict of Calvaryd enters with Him into the kingdom of heaven.
For the kingdom of heaven belongs to intelligence and love, both children of liberty.
God has shown liberty to man in the image of a lovely woman, and in order to test his courage, Hee the phantom of death pass between her and him.
Man loved, and felt himself to be God; he gave for her what God had just bestowed upon him --- etl hope.
He leapt towards his bride across the shadow of death.
Man possessed liberty; he had embraced life.
Expiate now thy glory, O Prometheus!
Thy heart, ceaselessly devoured, cannot die; it is thy vulture, it is Jupiter, who will die!
One day we shall awake at last from the painful dreams of a tormented life; our ordeal will be fied, and we shall be sufficiently strong against sorrow to be immortal.
Then we shall live in God with a more abundant life, and we shall descend into His works with theht of His thought, we shall be borne away into the infinite by the whisper of His love.
We shall be without doubt the elder brethren of a new race, the angels of posterity.
Celestial messengers, we shall wander in immensity, and the stars will be our gleaming ships. {18 We shall transform ourselves into sweet visions to calm weeping eyes; we shall gather radiant lilsi unknown meadows, and we shall scatter their dew upon the earth.
We shall touch the eyelid of the sleeping child, and rejoice the heart of its mother with the spele of the beauty of her well-beloved son!

II

THE BINARY

THE binary is more particularly the number of woman, mate of man and mother of society.
Man is love in intelligence; woman is intelligence in love.
Woman is the smile of the Creator content with himself, and it is after making her that He restedys the divine parable.
Woman stands before man because she is mother, and all is forgiven her in advance, because she br forth in sorrow.
Woman initiated herself first into immortality through death; then man saw her to be so beautifuld understood her to be so generous, that he refused to survive her, and loved her more than his lf,mre than his eternal happiness.
Happy outlaw, since she has been given to him as companion in his exile!
But the children of Cain have revolted against the mother of Abel; they have enslaved their mothe The beauty of woman has become a prey for the brutality of such men as cannot love.
Thus woman closed her heart as if it were a secret sanctuary, and said to men unworthy of her: "Ivirgin, {19} but I will to become mother, and my son will teach you to love me."
O Eve! Salutation and adoration in thy fall!
O Mary! Blessings and adoration in thy sufferings and in thy glory!
Crucified and holy one who didst survive thy God that thou mightst bury thy son, be thou for us tinal word of the divine revelation!
Moses called God "Lord"; Jesus called Him "My Father," and we, thinking of thee, may say to Provie, "You are our mother."
Children of woman, let us forgive fallen woman!
Children of woman, let us adore regenerate woman!
Children of woman, who have slept upon her breast, been cradled in her arms, and consoled by her sses, let us love her, and let us love each other!

III

THE TERNARY

THE Ternary is the number of creation.
God creates Himself eternally, and the infinite which He fills with His works is an incessant andinite creation.
Supreme love contemplates itself in beauty as in a mirror, and It essays all forms as adornments, It is the lover of life.
Man also affirms himself and creates himself; he adorns himself with his trophies of victory, he ghtens himself with his own conceptions, he clothes himself with his works as with a wedding garmn.{0}
The great week of creation has been imitated by human genius, divining the forms of nature.
Every day has furnished a new revelation, every new king of the world has been for a day the imagd the incarnation of God! Sublime dream which explains the mysteries of India, and justifies allsmoisms!
The lofty conception of the man-God corresponds to the creation of Adam, and Christianity, like tirst days of man in the earthly paradise, has been only an aspiration and a widowhood.
We wait for the worship of the bride and of the mother; we shall aspire to the wedding of the Newenant.
Then the poor, the blind, the outlaws of the old world will be invited to the feast, and will rec a wedding garment. They will gaze the one upon the other with inexpressible tenderness and a sml ht is ineffable because they have wept so long.

IV

THE QUATERNARY

THE Quaternary is the number of force. It is the ternary completed by its product, the rebelliouity reconciled to the sovereign trinity.
In the first fury of life, man, having forgotten his mother, no longer understood God but as an ixible and jealous father.
The sombre Saturn, armed with his parricidal scythe, set himself to devour his children.
Jupiter had eyebrows which shook Olympus; Jehovah wielded thunders which deafened the solitudes onai. {21}
Nevertheless, the father of men, being on occasion drunken like Noah, let the world perceive the eries of life.
Psyche, made divine by her torments, became the bride of Eros; Adonis, raised from death, found a his Venus in Olympus; Job, victorious over evil, recovered more than he had lost.
The law is a test of courage.
To love life more than one fears the menaces of death is to merit life.
The elect are those who dare; woe to the timid!
Thus the slaves of law, who make themselves the tyrants of conscience and the servants of fear, ahose who begrudge that man should hope, and the Pharisees of all the synagogues and of all the chrhs are those who receive the reproofs and the curses of the Father.
Was not the Christ excommunicated and crucified by the synagogue?
Was not Savonarola burned by the order of the sovereign pontiff of the Christian religion?
Are not the Pharisees to-day just what they were in the time of Caiaphas?
If any one speaks to them in the name of intelligence and love, will they listen?
In rescuing the children of liberty from the tyranny of the Pharaohs, Moses inaugurated the reignthe Father.
In breaking the insupportable yoke of mosaic pharisaism, Jesus welcomed all men to the brotherhoo the only son of God.
When the last ideals fall, when the last material chains of conscience break, when the last of thhat killed the {22} prophets and the last of them that stifled the Word are confounded, then willb h reign of the Holy Ghost.
Then, Glory to the Father who drowned the host of Pharaoh in the Red Sea!
Glory to the Son, who tore the veil of the temple, and whose cross, overweighing the crown of thesars, broke the forehead of the Caesars against the earth!
Glory to the Holy Ghost, who shall sweep from the earth by His terrible breath all the thieves anl the executioners, to make room for the banquet of the children of God!
Glory to the Holy Ghost, who has promised victory over earth and over heaven to the angel of libe
The angel of liberty was born before the dawn of the first day, before even the awakening of inteence, and God called him the morning star.
O Lucifer! Voluntarily and disdainfully thou didst detach thyself from the heaven where the sun ned thee in his splendour, to plow with thine own rays the unworked fields of night!
Thou shinest when the sun sets, and thy sparkling gaze precedes the daybreak!
Thou fallest to rise again; thou tastest of death to understand life better!
For the ancient glories of the world, thou art the evening star; for truth renascent, the lovely of dawn.
Liberty is not licence, for licence is tyranny.
Liberty is the guardian of duty, because it reclaims right.<<Right --- 'droit' --- a word much indence at the time, with no true English equivalent, save in such phrases as 'the right to work.' B telf it is only used in the plural, which will not do here, and throughout this treatise. --- TAN.>
Lucifer, of whom the dark ages have made the genius of {23} evil, will be truly the angel of lighen, having conquered liberty at the price of infamy, he will make use of it to submit himself to tra order, inaugurating thus the glories of voluntary obedience.
Right is only the root of duty; one must possess in order to give.
This is how a lofty and profound poetry explains the fall of the angels.
God hath given to His spirits light and life; then He said to them: "Love!"
"What is --- to love?" replied the spirits.
"To love is to give oneself to others," replied God. "Those who love will suffer, but they will oved."
"We have the right to give nothing, and we wish to suffer nothing," said the spirits, hating love "Remain in your right," answered God, "and let us separate! I and Mine wish to suffer and even tde to love. It is our duty!"
The fallen angel is then he who, from the beginning, refused to love; he does not love, and that is whole torture; he does not give, and that is his poverty; he does not suffer, and that is his ohnness; he does not die, and that is his exile.
The fallen angel is not Lucifer the light-bearer; it is Satan, who calumniated love.
To be rich is to give; to give nothing is to be poor; to live is to love; to love nothing is to bad; to be happy is to devote oneself; to exist only for oneself is to cast away oneself, and to eieoeself in hell.
Heaven is the harmony of generous thoughts; hell is the conflict of cowardly instincts. {24}
The man of right is Cain who kills Abel from envy; the man of duty is Abel who dies for Cain for .
And such has been the mission of Christ, the great Abel of humanity.
It is not for right that we should dare all, it is for duty.
Duty is the expansion and the enjoyment of liberty; isolated right is the father of slavery.
Duty is devotion; right is selfishness.
Duty is sacrifice; right is theft and rapine.
Duty is love, and right is hate.
Duty is infinite life; right is eternal death.
If one must fight to conquer right, it is only to acquire the power of duty: what use have we foredom, unless to love and to devote ourselves to God?
If one must break the law, it is when law imprisons love in fear.
"He that saveth his life shall lose it," says the holy Book; "and he who consents to lose it wille it."
Duty is love; perish every obstacle to love! Silence, ye oracles of hate! Destruction to the fagods of selfishness and fear! Shame to the slaves, the misers of love!
God loves prodigal children!

V

THE QUINARY

THE Quinary is the number of religion, for it is the number of God united to that of woman.<<Almooo visible a sneer of the Atheist and woman-despiser. --- O.M.>> {25}
Faith is not the stupid credulity of an awestruck ignorance.
Faith is the consciousness and the confidence of Love.
Faith is the cry of reason, which persists in denying the absurd, even in the presence of the unk.
Faith is a sentiment necessary to the soul, just as breathing is to life; it is the dignity of coe, and the reality of enthusiasm.
Faith does not consist of the affirmation of this symbol or that, but of a genuine and constant aation towards the truths which are veiled by all symbolisms.
If a man rejects an unworthy idea of divinity, breaks its false images, revolts against hateful iters, you will call him an atheist!
The authors of the persecutions in fallen Rome called the first Christians atheists, because they not adore the idols of Caligula or of Nero.
To deny a religion, even to deny all religions rather than adhere to formulae which conscience res, is a courageous and sublime act of faith. Every man who suffers for his convictions is a martro aith.
He explains himself badly, it may be, but he prefers justice and truth to everything; do not condhim without understanding him.
To believe in the supreme truth is not to define it, and to declare that one believes in it is toognize that one does not know it.
The Apostle St. Paul declares all faith contained in these two things: --- To believe that God isd that He rewards them who seek Him. {26}
Faith is a greater thing than all religions, because it states the articles of belief with less psion.
Any dogma constitutes but a belief, and belongs to our particular communion; faith is a sentimentch is common to the whole of humanity.
The more one discusses with the object of obtaining greater accuracy, the less one believes; everw dogma is a belief which a sect appropriates to itself, and thus, in some sort, steals from univra aith.
Let us leave sectarians to make and remake their dogmas; let us leave the superstitious to detail formulate their superstitions. As the Master said, "Let the dead bury their dead!" Let us beliv nthe indicible truth; let us believe in that Absolute which reason admits without understandingit lt s believe in what we feel without knowing it!
Let us believe in the supreme reason!
Let us believe in Infinite Love, and pity the stupidities of scholasticism and the barbarities ofse religion!
O man! Tell me what thou hopest, and I will tell thee what thou art worth.
Thou dost pray, thou dost fast, thou dost keep vigil; dost thou then believe that so thou wilt es alone, or almost alone, from the enormous ruin of mankind --- devoured by a jealous God? Thou atipous, and a hypocrite.
Dost thou turn life into an orgie, and hope for the slumber of nothingness? Thou art sick, and isate.
Art thou ready to suffer as others and for others, and hope for the salvation of all? Thou art ae and just man.
To hope is to fear not.
To be afraid of God, what blasphemy! {27}
The act of hope is prayer.
Prayer is the flowering of the soul in eternal wisdom and in eternal love.
It is the gaze of the spirit towards truth, and the sigh of the heart towards supreme beauty.
It is the smile of the child upon its mother.
It is the murmur of the lover, who reaches out towards the kisses of his mistress.
It is the soft joy of a loving soul as it expands in an ocean of love.
It is the sadness of the bride in the absence of the bridegroom.
It is the sigh of the traveller who thinks of his fatherland.
It is the thought of the poor man who works to support his wife and children.
Let us pray in silence; let us raise toward our unknown Father a look of confidence and of love; us accept with faith and resignation the part which He assigns to us in the toils of life, and evr hob of our hearts will be a word of prayer!
Have we need to inform God of what we ask from Him? Does not He know what is necessary for us?
If we weep, let us offer Him our tears; if we rejoice, let us turn towards Him our smile; if He s us, let us bow the head; if He caress us, let us sleep within His arms!
Our prayer will be perfect, when we pray without knowing whom we pray.
Prayer is not a noise which strikes the ear; it is a silence which penetrates the heart. {28}
Soft tears come to moisten the eyes, and sighs escape like incense smoke.
One feels oneself in love, ineffably in love, with all that is beauty, truth, and justice; one th with a new life, and one fears no more to die. For prayer is the eternal life of intelligence adlv; it is the life of God upon earth.
Love one another --- that is the Law and the Prophets! Meditate, and understand this word.
And when you have understood, read no more, seek no more, doubt no more --- love!
Be no more wise, be no more learned --- love! That is the whole doctrine of true religion; religmeans charity, and God Himself is only love.
I have already said to you, to love is to give.
The impious man is he who absorbs others.
The pious man is he who loses himself in humanity.
If the heart of man concentrate in himself the fire with which God animates it, it is a hell whicvours all, and fills itself only with ashes; if he radiates it without, it becomes a tender sun o oe
Man owes himself to his family; his family owes itself to the fatherland; and the fatherland to hity.
The egoism of man merits isolation and despair; that of the family, ruin and exile; that of the frland, war and invasion.
The man who isolates himself from every human love, saying, "I will serve God," deceives himself.r, said St. John the Apostle, if he loveth not his neighbour whom he hath see, how shall he love o hm he hath not seen?
One must render to God that which is God's, but one must not refuse even to Caesar that which is ar's. {29}
God is He who gives life; Caesar can only give death.
One must love God, and not fear Caesar; as it is written in the Holy Book, "He that taketh the swshall perish by the sword."
You wish to be good? Then be just. You wish to be just? Then be free.
The vices which make man like the brute are the first enemies of his liberty.
Consider the drunkard, and tell me if this unclean brute can be called free!
The miser curses the life of his father, and, like the crow, hungers for corpses.
The goal of the ambitious man is --- ruins; it is the delirium of envy! The debauchee spits upon breast of his mother, and fills with abortions the entrails of death.
All these loveless hearts are punished by the most cruel of all tortures, hate.
Because --- take it to heart! --- the expiation is implicit in the sin.
The man who does evil is like an earthen pot ill-made; he will break himself: fatality wills it.
With the debris of the worlds, God makes stars; with the debris of souls He makes angels.

VI

THE SENARY

THE Senary is the number of initiation by ordeal; it is the number of equilibrium, it is the hierph of the knowledge of Good and Evil. {30}
He who seeks the origin of evil, seeks the source of what is not.
Evil is the disordered appetite of good, the unfruitful attempt of an unskilful will.
Every one possesses the fruit of his work, and poverty is only the spur to toil.
For the flock of men, suffering is like the shepherd dog, who bites the wool of the sheep to put back in the right way.
It is because of shadow that we are able to see light; because of cold that we feel heat; becausepain that we are sensible to pleasure.
Evil is then for us the occasion and the beginning of good.
But, in the dreams of our imperfect intelligence, we accuse the work of Providence, through failio understand it.
We resemble the ignorant person who judges the picture by the beginning of the sketch, and says, the head is done, "What! Has this figure no body?"
Nature remains calm, and accomplishes its work.
The ploughshare is not cruel when it tears the bosom of the earth, and the great revolutions of torld are the husbandry of God.
There is a place for everything: to savage peoples, barbarous masters; to cattle, butchers; to meudges and fathers.
If time could change the sheep into lions, they would eat the butchers and the shepherds.
Sheep never change because they do not instruct themselves; but peoples instruct themselves.
Shepherds and butchers of the people, you are then {31} right to regard as your enemies those whoak to your flock!
Flocks who know yet only your shepherds, and who wish to remain ignorant of their dealings with tutchers, it is excusable that you should stone them who humiliate you and disturb you, in speakin oyu of your rights.
O Christ! The authorities condemn Thee, Thy disciples deny Thee, the people curses Thee, and dem Thy murder; only Thy mother weeps for Thee, even God abandons Thee!
"Eli! Eli! lama sabachthani!"

VII

THE SEPTENARY

THE Septenary is the great biblical number. It is the key of the Creation in the books of Moses the symbol of all religion. Moses left five books, and the Law is complete in two testaments.
The Bible is not a history, it is a collection of poems, a book of allegories and images.
Adam and Eve are only the primitive types of humanity; the tempter serpent is time which tests; tree of Knowledge is 'right'; the expiation by toil is duty.
Cain and Abel represent the flesh and the spirit, force and intelligence, violence and harmony.
The giants are those who usurped the earth in ancient times; the flood was a great revolution.
The ark is tradition preserved in a family: religion at this period becomes a mystery and the proy of the race. Ham was cursed for having revealed it. {32}
Nimrod and Babel are the two primitive allegories of the despot, and of the universal empire whics always filled the dreams of men, --- a dream whose fulfilment was sought successively by the Asyin, the Medes, the Persians, Alexander, Rome, Napoleon, the successors of Peter the Great, and awas nfnished because of the dispersion of interests, symbolized by the confusion of tongues.
The universal empire could not realize itself by force, but by intelligence and love. Thus, to Nd, the man of savage 'right,' the Bible opposed Abraham, the man of duty, who goes voluntarily inoeie in order to seek liberty and strife in a strange country, which he seizes by virtue of his "de." He has a sterile wife, his thought, and a fertile slave, his force; but when force has produced ifrut, thought becomes fertile; and the son of intelligence drives into exile the child of force. Teman f intelligence is submitted to rude tests; he must confirm his conquests by sacrifices. Godorer him o immolate his son, that is to say, doubt ought to test dogma, and the intellectual man houd b readyto sacrifice everything on the altar of supreme reason. Then God intervenes: universl reson ields t the efforts of labour, and shows herself to science; the material side of dogma i alon immoated. . This is the meaning of the ram caught by its horns in a thicket. The history o Abrahm is, hen, a sybol in the ancient manner, and contains a lofty revelation of the destinies f the hman sou. Taken lterally, it is an absurd and revolting story. Did not St. Augustine takeliterall the Golen Ass of Auleius?
Poor great men! {33}
The history of Isaac is another legend. Rebecca is the type of the oriental woman, laborious, htable, partial in her affections, shrewd and wily in her manoeuvres. Jacob and Esau are again th w ypes of Cain and Abel; but here Abel avenges himself: the emancipated intelligence triumphs bycunig. The whole of the genius of the Jews is in the character of Jacob, the patient and laboriou suplaterwho yields to the wrath of Esau, becomes rich, and buys his brother's forgiveness. One ust everforgt that, when the ancients want to philosophize, they tell a story.
The history or legend of Joseph contains, in germ, the whole genius of the Gospel; and the Christsunderstood by His people, must often have wept in reading over again that scene, where the Govero fEgypt throws himself on the neck of Benjamin, with the great cry of "I am Joseph!"
Israel becomes the people of God, that is to say, the conservator of the idea, and the depositarif the word. This idea is that of human independence, and of royalty, by means of work; but one hdsi with care, like a precious seed. A painful and indelible sign is imprinted on the initiates;evryimge of the truth is forbidden, and the children of Jacob watch, sword in hand, around the unty f te tbernacle. Hamor and Shechem wish to introduce themselves forcibly into the holy family,and eris wit their people after undergoing a feigned initiation. In order to dominate the vulgar it i alredy neessary that the sanctuary should surround itself with sacrifices and with terror.
The servitude of the children of Jacob paves the way for their deliverance: for they have an idead one does not enchain an idea; they have a religion, and one does not {34} violate a religion; te r, in fine, a people, and one does not enchain a real people. Persecution stirs up avengers; te de icarnates itself in a man; Moses springs up; Pharaoh falls; and the column of smoke and flam, wichgoe before a freed people, advances majestically into the desert.
Christ is priest and king by intelligence and by love.
He has received the holy unction, the unction of genius, faith and virtue, which is force.
He comes when the priesthood is worn out, when the old symbols have no more virtue, when the beacf intelligence is extinguished.
He comes to recall Israel to life, and if he cannot galvanize Israel, slain by the Pharisees, intfe, he will resurrect the world given over to the dead worship of idols.
Christ is the right to do one's duty.
Man has the right to do his duty, and he has no other right.
O man! thou hast the right to resist even unto death any who prevents thee from doing thy duty.
Mother! Thy child is drowning; a man prevents thee from helping him; thou strikest this man, thost run to save thy son! ... Who, then, will dare to condemn thee?
Christ came to oppose the right of duty to the duty of right.
'Right,' with the Jews, was the doctrine of the Pharisees. And, indeed, they seemed to have acqu the privilege of dogmatizing; were they not the legitimate heirs of the synagogue?
They had the right to condemn the Saviour, and the Saviour knew that His duty was to resist them.}
Christ is the soul of protest.
But the protest of what? Of the flesh against the intelligence? No!
Of right against duty? No!
Of the physical against the moral? No! No!
Of imagination against universal reason? Of folly against wisdom? No, a thousand times No, and more No!
Christ is the reality, duty, which protests eternally against the ideality, right.
He is the emancipation of the spirit which breaks the slavery of the flesh.
He is devotion in revolt against egoism.
He is the sublime modesty which replies to pride: "I will not obey thee!"
Christ is unmated; Christ is solitary; Christ is sad: Why?
Because woman has prostituted herself.
Because society is guilty of theft.
Because selfish joy is impious.
Christ is judged, condemned, and executed; and men adore Him!
This happened in a world perhaps as serious as our own.
Judges of the world in which we live, pay attention, and think of Him who will judge your judgmen But, before dying, the Saviour bequeathed to His children the immortal sign of salvation, Communi Communion! Common union! the final word of the Saviour of the world!
"The Bread and the Wine shared among all," said He, "this is my flesh and my blood." {36}
He gave His flesh to the executioners, His blood to the earth which drank it. Why?
In order that all may partake of the bread of intelligence, and of the wine of love.
O sign of the union of men! O Round Table of universal chivalry! O banquet of fraternity and eqty! When will you be better understood?
Martyrs of humanity, all ye who have given your life in order that all should have the bread whicurishes and the wine which fortifies, do ye not also say, placing your hands on the signs of the nvral communion: "This is our flesh and our blood"?
And you, men of the whole world, you whom the Master calls His brothers; oh, do you not feel that universal bread, the fraternal bread, the bread of the communion, is God?
Retailers of the Crucified One!
All you who are not ready to give your blood, your flesh and your life to humanity, you are not wy of the Communion of the Son of God! Do not let His blood flow upon you, for it would brand you oeead!
Do not approach your lips to the heart of God, He would feel your sting!
Do not drink the blood of the Christ, it will burn your entrails; it is quite sufficient that it ld have flowed uselessly for you!

VIII

THE NUMBER EIGHT

THE Ogdoad is the number of reaction and of equilibrating justice. {37}
Every action produces a reaction.
This is the universal law of the world.
Christianity must needs produce anti-Christianity.
Antichrist is the shadow, the foil, the proof of Christ.
Antichrist already produced itself in the Church in the time of the Apostles: St. Paul said: --- the mystery of iniquity doth already work; only he who now letteth will let, until he be taken oto he way. And then shall that Wicked One be revealed. ..."<<2 Thess. ii. 7,8. This passage is pesmalythat referred to by the author. Cf. 1 John iv. 3, and ii, 18. --- TRANS.>>
The Protestants said: "Antichrist is the Pope."
The Pope replied: "Every heretic is an Antichrist."
The Antichrist is no more the Pope than Luther; the Antichrist is the spirit opposed to that of Ct.
It is the usurpation of right for the sake of right; it is the pride of domination and the despotof thought.
It is the selfishness, self-styled religious, of Protestants, as well as the credulous and imperiignorance of bad Catholics.
The Antichrist is what divides men instead of uniting them; it is a spirit of dispute, the obstinof the theologians and sectarians, the impious desire of appropriating the truth to oneself, and xldng others from it, or of forcing the whole world to submit to the narrow yoke of our judgments
The Antichrist is the priest who curses instead of blessing, who drives away instead of attractingo scandalizes instead of edifying, who damns instead of saving.
It is the hateful fanaticism which discourages good-will.
It is the worship of death, sadness, and ugliness. {38}
"What career shall we choose for our son?" have said many stupid parents; "he is mentally and bodweak, and he is without a spark of courage: --- we will make a priest of him, so that he may 'liv yte altar.'" They have not understood that the altar is not a manger for slothful animals.
Look at the unworthy priests, contemplate these pretended servants of the altar! What do they sa your heart, these obese or cadaverous men with the lack-lustre eyes, and pinched or gaping mouth?<cual priests. Levi's ideal priest, of whom 'bad; is an impossible epithet, is not to be looked orinth Church. He is in that 'Church' which is also Ark, Rose, Font, Altar, Cup, and the rest. e i tht Wrd of Truth which is 'established' by two witnesses. --- O. M.>>
Hear them talk: what does it teach you, their disagreeable and monotonous noise?
They pray as they sleep, and they sacrifice as they eat.
They are machines full of bread, meat and wine, and of senseless words.
And when they plume themselves, like the oyster in the sun, on being without thought and without , one says that they have peace of soul!
They have the peace of the brute. For man, that of the tomb is better: these are the priests of y and ignorance, these are the ministers of Antichrist.
The true priest of Christ is a man who lives, suffers, loves and fights for justice. He does notpute, he does not reprove; he sends out pardon, intelligence and love.
The true Christian is a stranger to the sectarian spirit; he is all things to all men, and looks ll men as the children of a common father, who means to save them all. The whole cult has for hi nya sense of sweetness and of {39} love: he leaves to God the secrets of justice, and understand olychrity.
He looks on the wicked as invalids whom one must pity and cure; the world, with its errors and vi is to him God's hospital, and he wishes to serve in it.
He does not think that he is better than any one else; he says only, "So long as I am in good hea let me serve others; and when I must fall and die, perhaps others will take my place and serve."

IX

THE NUMBER NINE

THIS is the hermit of the Tarot; the number which refers to initiates and to prophets.
The prophets are solitaries, for it is their fate that none should ever hear them.
They see differently from others; they forefeel misfortunes. So, people imprison them and kill t or mock them, repulse them as if they were lepers, and leave them to die of hunger.
Then, when the predictions come true, they say, "It is these people who have brought us misfortun Now, as is always the case on the eve of great disasters,<<This is the true clairvoyant Levi. Thvi who prophesied Universal Empire for Napoleon III was either the Magus trying to use him as a to,o a Micaiah unadjured. --- O. M.>> our streets are full of prophets.
I have met some of them in the prisons, I have seen others who were dying forgotten in garrets.
The whole great city has seen one of them whose silent {40} prophecy was to turn ceaselessly as hlked, covered with rags, in the palace of luxury and riches.
I have seen one of them whose face shone like that of Christ: he had callosities on his hands, anre the workman's blouse; with clay he kneaded epics. He twisted together the sword of right and h cptre of duty; and upon this column of gold and steel he placed the creative sign of love.
One day, in a great popular assembly, he went down into the road with a piece of bread in his hanich he broke and distributed, saying: "Bread of God, do thou make bread for all!"
I know another of them who cried: "I will no longer adore the god of the devil! I will not have ngman for my God!" And they thought that he blasphemed.
No; but the energy of his faith overflowed in inexact and imprudent words.
He said again in the madness of his wounded charity: "The liabilities of all men are common, and expiate each other's faults, as they make merit for each other by their virtues.
"The penalty of sin is death.
"Sin itself, moreover, is a penalty, and the greatest of penalties. A great crime is nothing butreat misfortune.
"The worst of men is he who thinks himself better than his follows.
"Passionate men are excusable, because they are passive; passion means suffering, and also redemp through sorrow.
"What we call liberty is nothing but the all-mightiness of divine compulsion. The martyrs said: is better to obey God than man'." {41}
"The least perfect act of love is worth more than the best act of piety."
"Judge not; speak hardly at all; love and act."
Another prophet came and said: "Protest against bad doctrines by good works, but do not separate selves.
"Rebuild all the altars, purify all the temples, and hold yourselves in readiness for the visit oe Spirit.
"Let every one pray in his own fashion, and hold communion with his own; but do not condemn other "A religious practice is never contemptible, for it is the sign of a great and holy thought.
"To pray together is to communicate in the same hope, the same faith,and the same charity.
"The sign by itself is nothing; it is the faith which sanctifies it.
"Religion is the most sacred and the strongest bond of human association, and to perform an act oligion is to perform an act of humanity."
When men understand at last that one must not dispute about things about which one is ignorant,
When they feel that a little charity is worth more than much influence and domination,
When the whole world respects what even God respects in the least of His creatures, the spontaneif obedience and the liberty of duty,
Then there will be no more than one religion in the world, the Christian and universal religion, true Catholic religion, which will no longer deny itself by restrictions of place and of persons. Wman," said the Saviour to the woman of Samaria, {42} "Verily I say unto thee, that the time coet wenmen shall no longer worship God, either in Jerusalem, or on this mountain; for God is a spiit,<A istanslation by monotheists.
