THE ABSOLUTE NUMBER OF THE QABALAH

THE key of the Sephiroth. (Vide "Dogme et rituel de la haute magie.")

XI

THE NUMBER ELEVEN

ELEVEN is the number of force; it is that of strife and martyrdom.
Every man who dies for an idea is a martyr, for in him the aspirations of the spirit have triumphver the fears of the animal.
Every man who falls in war is a martyr, for he dies for others.
Every man who dies of starvation is a martyr, for he is like a soldier struck down in the battle ife.
Those who die in defence of right are as holy in their sacrifice as the victims of duty, and in treat struggles and revolutions against power, martyrs fell equally on both sides.
Right being the root of duty, our duty is to defend our rights.
What is a crime? The exaggeration of a right. Murder {43} and theft are negations of society; i the isolated despotism of an individual who usurps royalty, and makes war at his own risk and pei. Crime should doubtless be repressed, and society must defend itself; but who is so just, so greato ure, as to pretend that he has the right to punish?
Peace then to all who fall in war, even in unlawful war! For they have staked their heads and thave lost them; they have paid, and what more can we ask of them?
Honour to all those who fight bravely and loyally! Shame only on the traitors and cowards!
Christ died between two thieves, and He took one of them with Him to heaven.
The Kingdom of Heaven suffereth violence, and the violent take it by force.
God bestows His almighty power on love. He loves to triumph over hate, but the lukewarm He spuetrth from His mouth.
Duty is to live, were it but for an instant!
It is fine to have reigned for a day, even for an hour! though it were beneath the sword of Damoc or upon the pyre of Sardanapalus!
But it is finer to have seen at one's feet all the crowns of the world, and to have said, "I willthe king of the poor, and my throne shall be on Calvary."
There is one man stronger than the man that slays; it is he who dies to save others.
There are no isolated crimes and no solitary expiations.
There are no personal virtues, nor are there any wasted devotions. {44}
Whoever is not without reproach is the accomplice of all evil; and whoever is not absolutely perv, may participate in all good.
For this reason an agony is always an humanitarian expiation, and every head that falls upon the fold may be honoured and praised as the head of a martyr.
For this reason also, the noblest and the holiest of martyrs could inquire of his own conscience,d himself deserving of the penalty that he was about to undergo, and say, saluting the sword thatwsrady to strike him, "Let justice be done!"
Pure victims of the Roman Catacombs, Jews and Protestants massacred by unworthy Christians!
Priests of l'Abbaye and les Carmes,<<Monasteries in Paris which were used as prisons in the ReignTerror. --- TRANS.>> victims of the Reign of Terror, butchered royalists, revolutionaries sacrifie nyour turn, soldiers of our great armies who have sown the world with your bones, all you who hvesufeed the penalty of death, workers, strivers, darers of every kind, brave children of Promethus,whohav feared neither the lightning nor the vulture, all honour to your scattered ashes! Peac andveneatio to your memories! You are the heroes of progress, martyrs of humanity!

XII

THE NUMBER TWELVE

TWELVE is the cyclic number; it is that of the universal Creed. {45}
Here is a translation in alexandrines of the unrestricted magical and Catholic creed: ---

I do believe in God, almighty sire of man.
One God, who did create the universe, his plan.

I do believe in Him, the Son, the chief of men,
Word and magnificence of the supreme Amen.

He is the living thought of Love's eternal might,
God manifest in flesh, the Action of the Light.

Desired in every place and every period,
But not a God that one may separate from God.

Descended among men to free the earth from fate,
He in His mother did the woman consecrate.

He was the man whom heaven's sweet wisdom did adorn;
To suffer and to die as men do He was born.

Proscribed by ignorance, accused by envy and strife,
He died upon the cross that He might give us life.

All who accept His aid to guide and to sustain
By His example may to God like Him attain.

He rose from death to reign throughout the ages' dance;
He is the sun that melts the clouds of ignorance.

His precepts, better known and mightier soon to be,
Shall judge the quick and dead for all eternity.

I do believe in God's most Holy Spirit, whose fire
The heart and mind of saints and prophets did inspire.

He is a Breath of life and of fecundity,
Proceeding both from God and from humanity.

I do believe in one most holy brotherhood
Of just men that revere heaven's ordinance of good.

I do believe one place, one pontiff, and one right,
One symbol of one God, in one intent unite.

I do believe that death by changing us renews,
And that in man as God life sheds immortal dews.

XIII

THE NUMBER THIRTEEN

THIRTEEN is the number of death and of birth; it is that of property and of inheritance, of sociend of family, of war and of treaties. {46}
The basis of society is the exchange of right, duty and good faith.
Right is property, exchange is necessity, good faith is duty.
He who wants to receive more than he gives, or who wants to receive without giving, is a thief.
Property is the right to dispose of a portion of the common wealth; it is not the right to destroor the right to sequestrate.
To destroy or sequestrate the common wealth is not to possess; it is to steal.
I say common wealth, because the true proprietor of all things is God, who wishes all things to bg to everybody. Whatever you may do, at your death you will carry away nothing of this world's gos Now, that which must be taken away from you one day is not really yours. It has only been len t yu. As to the usufruct, it is the result of work; but even work is not an assured guarantee of posses, and war may come with devastation and fire to displace property.
Make then good use of those things which perish, O you who will perish before they do!
Consider that egoism provokes egoism, and that the immorality of the rich man will answer for themes of the poor.
What does the poor man wish, if he is honest? He wishes for work.
Use your rights, but do your duty: the duty of the rich man is to spread wealth; wealth which doet circulate is dead; do not hoard death!
A sophist<<Proudhon. --- TRANS.>> has said, "Property is robbery," and he {47} doubtless wished teak of property absorbed in itself, withdrawn from free exchange, turned from common use.
If such were his thought, he might go further, and say that such a suppression of public life is ed assassination.
It is the crime of monopoly, which public instinct has always looked upon as treason to the humane.
The family is a natural society which results from marriage.
Marriage is the union of two beings joined by love, who promise each other mutual devotion in theerest of the children who may be born.
Married persons who have a child, and who separate, are impious. Do they then wish to execute thdgment of Solomon and hew the child asunder?
To vow eternal love is puerile; sexual love is an emotion, divine doubtless, but accidental, invoary and transitory; but the promise of reciprocal devotion is the essence of marriage and the funaetl principle of the family.
The sanction and the guarantee of this promise must then be an absolute confidence.
Every jealousy is a suspicion, and every suspicion is an outrage.
The real adultery is the breach of this trust: the woman who complains of her husband to another the man who confides to another woman the disappointments or the hopes of his heart, --- these d,ided, betray conjugal faith.
The surprises which one's senses spring upon one are only infidelities on account of the impulsesthe heart which abandons itself more or less to the whispers of pleasure. Moreover, these are hua alts for which one must blush, {48} and which one ought to hide: they are indecencies which onemut vod in advance by removing opportunity, but which one must never seek to surprise: morality poscibe scndal.
Every scandal is a turpitude. One is not indecent because one possesses organs which modesty doet name, but one is obscene when one exhibits them.
Husbands, hide your domestic wounds; do not strip your wives naked before the laughter of the mob Women, do not advertise the discomforts of the conjugal bed: to do so is to write yourselves prostts in public opinion.
It needs a lofty degree of courage to keep conjugal faith; it is a pact of heroism of which only t souls can understand the whole extent.
Marriages which break are not marriages: they are couplings.
A woman who abandons her husband, what can she become? She is no more a wife, and she is not a w; what is she then? She is an apostate from honour who is forced to be licentious because she isnihr virgin nor free.
A husband who abandons his wife prostitutes her, and deserves the infamous name that one applies he lovers of lost women.
Marriage is then sacred and indissoluble when it really exists.
But it cannot really exist, except for beings of a lofty intelligence and of a noble heart.
The animals do not marry, and men who live like animals undergo the fatalities of the brute natur They ceaselessly make unfortunate attempts to act {49} reasonably. Their promises are attempts atdimitations of promises; their marriages, attempts at and imitations of marriage; their loves, ateps t and imitations of love. They always wish, and never will; they are always undertaking and evr ompeting. For such people, only the repressive side of law applies.
Such beings may have a litter, but they never have a family: marriage and family are the rights oe perfect man, the emancipated man, the man who is intelligent and free.
Ask also the annals of the Courts, and read the history of parricides.
Raise the black veil from off all those chopped heads, and ask them what they thought of marriage of the family, what milk they sucked, what caresses ennobled them. ... Then shudder, all you whod o give to your children the bread of intelligence and of love, all you who do not sanction patena athrity by the virtue of a good example!
Those wretches were orphans in spirit and in heart, and they have avenged their birth.
We live in a century when more than ever the family is misunderstood in all that it possesses whiartakes of the august and the sacred: material interest is killing intelligence and love; the lesoso experience are despised, the things of God are hawked about the street. The flesh insults th sirt,fraud laughs in the face of loyalty. No more idealism, no more justice: human life has murere boh is father and its mother.
Courage and patience! This century will go where great criminals should go. Look at it, how sadis! Weariness {50} is the black veil of its face ... the tumbril rolls on, and the shuddering crw olows it. ..
Soon one more century will be judged by history, and one will write upon a mighty tomb of ruins:
"Here ends the parricide century! The century which murdered its God and its Christ!"
In war, one has the right to kill, in order not to die: but in the battle of life the most sublim rights is that of dying in order not to kill.
Intelligence and love should resist oppression unto death --- but never unto murder.
Brave man, the life of him who has offended you is in your hands; for he is master of the life ofers who cares not for his own... Crush him beneath your greatness: pardon him!
"But is it forbidden to kill the tiger which threatens us?"
"If it is a tiger with a human face, it is finer to let him devour us, --- yet, for all that, mory has here nothing to say."
"But if the tiger threatens my children?"
"Let Nature herself reply to you!"
Harmodius and Aristogiton had festivals and statues in Ancient Greece. The Bible has consecrated names of Judith and Ehud, and one of the most sublime figures of the Holy Book is that of Samson,bidand chained, pulling down the columns of the temple, as he cried: "Let me die with the Philistne!" And yet, do you think that, if Jesus, before dying, had gone to Rome to plunge his dagger in the rt f Tiberius, He would have saved the world, as He did, in forgiving His executioners, and in dyn or een Tiberius? {51}
Did Brutus save Roman liberty by killing Caesar? In killing Caligula, Chaerea only made place foaudius and Nero. To protest against violence by violence, is to justify it, and to force it to rpoue itself.
But to triumph over evil by good, over selfishness by selfabnegation, over ferocity by pardon, ths the secret of Christianity, and it is that of eternal victory.
"I have seen the place where the earth still bled from the murder of "Abel," and on that place thran a brook of tears.
Under the guidance of the centuries, myriads of men moved on, letting fall their tears into the b.
And Eternity, crouching mournful, gazed upon the tears which fell; she counted them one by one, ahere were never enough to them to wash away one stain of blood.
But between two multitudes and two ages came the Christ, a pale and radiant figure.
And in the earth of blood and tears, He planted the vine of fraternity; and the tears and the blosucked up by the roots of the divine tree, became the delicious sap of the grape, which is destindt ntoxicate with love the children of the future.

XIV

THE NUMBER FOURTEEN

FOURTEEN is the number of fusion, of association, and of universal unity, and it is in the name oat it represents that we shall here make an appeal to the nations, beginning with the most ancien n he most holy.
Children of Israel, why, in the midst of the movement of {52} the nations, do you rest immobile, dians of the tombs of your fathers?
Your fathers are not here, they are risen: for the God of Abraham, of Isaac, and of Jacob, is not God of the dead!
Why do you always impress upon your offspring the bloody sigil of the knife?
God no longer wishes to separate you from other men; be our brethren, and eat with us the consecr Bread of peace on altars that blood stains never.
The law of Moses is accomplished: read your books and understand that you have been a blind and hhearted race, even as all your prophets said to you.
You have also been a courageous race, a race that persevered in strife.
Children of Israel, become the children of God: Understand and love!
God has wiped from your forehead the brand of Cain, and the peoples seeing you pass will no longey, "There go the Jews!" They will cry, "Room for our brethren! Room for our elders in the Faith" nd we shall go every year to eat the passover with you in the city of the New Jerusalem.
And we shall take our rest under your vine and under your fig-tree; for you will be once more theend of the traveller, in memory of Abraham, of Tobias, and of the angels who visited them.
And in memory of Him who said: "He who receiveth the least of these My little ones, receiveth Me. For then you will no longer refuse an asylum in your {53} house and in your heart to your brotheroeh, whom you sold to the Gentiles.
Because he has become powerful in the land of Egypt where you sought bread in the days of famine.And he has remembered his father Jacob, and Benjamin his young brother, and he pardons you your jaos, and embraces you with tears.
Children of true believers, we will sing with you: "There is no God but God, and Mohammed is His het!"
Say with the children of Israel: "There is no God but God, and Moses is His prophet!"
Say with the Christians: "There is no God but God, and Jesus Christ is His prophet!"
Mohammed is the shadow of Moses. Moses is the forerunner of Jesus.
What is a prophet? A representative of humanity seeking God. God is God, and man is the prophetGod, when he causes us to believe in God.
The Old Testament, the Qur'an, and the Gospel are three different translations of the same book. God is one, so also is the law.
O ideal woman! O reward of the elect! Art thou more beautiful than Mary?
O Mary, daughter of the East! caste as pure love, great as the desire of motherhood, come and teahe children of Islam the mysteries of Paradise, and the secrets of beauty!
Invite them to the festival of the new alliance! There, upon three thrones glittering with precistones, three prophets will be seated. {54}
The tuba tree will make, with its back-curving branches, a dais for the celestial table.
The bride will be white as the moon, and scarlet as the smile of morning.
All nations shall press forward to see her, and they will no longer fear to pass AL Sirah; for, oat razor-edged bridge, the Saviour will stretch His cross, and come to stretch His hand to those h tmble, and to those who have fallen the bride will stretch her perfumed veil, and draw them to er
O ye people, clap your hands, and praise the last triumph of love! Death alone will remain dead, hell alone will be consumed!
O nations of Europe, to whom the East stretches forth its hands, unite and push back the northernr!<<Written about the time of the Crimean War, this indicates Levi's attempt to use Imperialism a i agical weapon, just as Allan Bennett tried to use Buddhism. Such will be the conquests of progress, such is the end towards which the whole movement of the w is pushing us.
Progress is movement, and movement is life.
To deny progress is to affirm nothingness, and to deify death.
Progress is the only reply that reason can give to the objections which the existence of evil rai {55}
All is not well, but all will be well one day. God begins His work, and He will finish it.
Without progress, evil would be immutable like God.
Progress explains ruins, and consoles the weeping of Jeremiah.
Nations succeed each other like men; and nothing is stable, because everything is marching towardrfection.
The great man who dies bequeathes to his country the fruit of his works; the great nation which bes extinguished upon earth transforms itself into a star to enlighten the obscurities of History. ht it has written by its actions remains graven in the eternal book; it has added a page to theBileofthe human race.
Do not say that civilization is bad; for it resembles the damp heat which ripens the harvest, it dly develops the principles of life and the principles of death, it kills and it vivifies.
It is like the angel of the judgment who separates the wicked from the good.
Civilization transforms men of good will into angels of light, and lowers the selfish man beneath brute; it is the corruption of bodies and the emancipation of souls.
The impious world of the giants raised to Heaven the soul of Enoch; above the Bacchanals of primi Greece rises the harmonious spirit of Orpheus.
Socrates and Pythagoras, Plato and Aristotle, resume, in explaining them, all the aspirations and the glories of the ancient world; the fables of Homer remain truer than history, and nothing remist us of the grandeur of Rome {56} but the immortal writings which the century of Augustus brougt orh. Thus, perhaps, Rome only shook the world with the convulsions of war, in order to bring forth Ver
Christianity is the fruit of the meditations of all the sages of the East, who live again in Jesurist.
Thus the light of the spirits has risen where the sun of the world rises; Christ conquered the Weand the soft rays of the sun of Asia have touched the icicles of the North.
Stirred by this unknown heat, ant-heaps of new men have spread over a worn-out world; the souls oad people have shone upon rejuvenated races, and enlarged in them the spirit of life.
There is in the world a nation which calls itself frankness and freedom, for these two words are nymous with the name of France.
This nation has always been in some ways more Catholic than the Pope, and more Protestant than Lu.
The France of the Crusades, the France of the Troubadours, the France of songs, the France of Rabs and of Voltaire, the France of Bossuet and of Pascal, it is she who is the synthesis of all peols t is she who consecrates the alliance of reason and of faith, of revolution and of power, of te os tnder belief and of the proudest human dignity.
And, see how she marches, how she swings herself, how she struggles, how she grows great!
Often deceived and wounded, never cast down, enthusiastic over her triumphs, daring in her adverss, she laughs, she sings, she dies, and she teaches the world faith in immortality. {57}
The old guard does not surrender, but neither does it die! The proof of it is the enthusiasm of children, who mean, one day, to be also soldiers of the old guard!
Napoleon is no more a man: he is the very genius of France, he is the second saviour of the worldd he also gave for a sign the cross to his apostles.
St. Helena and Golgotha are the beacons of the new civilization; they are the two piles of an imm bridge made by the rainbow of the final deluge, and which throws a bridge between the two worlds
And can you believe that a past without aureole and without glory, might capture and devour so gra future?
Could you think that the spur of a Tartar might one day tear up the pact of our glories, the testt of our liberties?
Say rather that we may again become children, and enter again into our mother's womb!
"Go on! Go on!" said the voice of God to the wandering Jew. "Advance! Advance!" the destiny of world cries out to France. And where do we go? To the unknown, the the abyss perhaps; no matter Btto the past, to the cemeteries of oblivion, to the swaddling-clothes which our childhood itsel treinshreds, towards the imbecility and ignorance of the earliest ages ... never! never!

XV

THE NUMBER FIFTEEN

FIFTEEN is the number of antagonism, and of catholicity.
Christianity is at present divided into two churches: the {58} civilizing church, and the savage ch; the progressive church, and the stationary church.
One is active, the other is passive: one has mastered the nations and governs them always, since s fear it; the other has submitted to every despotism, and can be nothing but an instrument of slvr. The active church realizes God for men, and alone believes in the divinity of the human Word, as nterpreter of that of God.
What after all is the infallibility of the Pope, but the autocracy of intelligence, confirmed by universal vote of faith?
In this case, one might say, the Pope ought to be the first genius of his century. Why? It is mproper, in reality, that he should be an average man. His supremacy is only more divine for that ease it is in a way more human.
Do not events speak louder than rancours and irreligious ignorances? Do not you see Catholic Frasustaining with one hand the tottering papacy, and with the other holding the sword to fight at teha of the army of progress?
Catholics, Jews, Turks, Protestants, already fight under the same banner; the crescent has rallie the Latin cross, and altogether we struggle against the invasion of the barbarians, and their brtlzng orthodoxy.
It is for ever an accomplished fact. In admitting new dogmas, the chair of St. Peter has solemnloclaimed itself progressive.
The fatherland of Catholic Christianity is that of the sciences and of the fine arts; and the ete Word of the Gospel, living and incarnate in a visible authority, is still the light of the world 5} Silence, then, to the Pharisees of the new synagogue! Silence to the hateful traditions of the Sls, to the arrogance of Presbyterianism, to the absurdity of Jansenism, and to all those shamefuladsperstitious interpretations of the eternal dogma, so justly stigmatized by the pitiless geniusofVotare!
Voltaire and Napoleon died Catholics.<<"I do not say that Voltaire died a good Catholic, but he da Catholic." --- E. L. It will be the dogma of the Gospel, tried like gold by the critical acid of Voltaire, and realizen the kingdom of the world, by the genius of the Christian Napoleon.
Those who will not march will be dragged or trampled by events.
Immense calamities may again hang over the world. The armies of the Apocalypse may, perhaps, one, unchain the four scourges. The sanctuary will be cleansed. Rigid and holy poverty will send frhis apostles to uphold what staggers, lift up again what is broken, and anoint all wounds with scrd il.
Those two blood-hungered monsters, despotism and anarchy, will tear themselves to pieces, and annate each other, after having mutually sustained each other for a little while, by the embrace of hi truggle itself.
And the government of the future will be that whose model is shown to us in nature, by the familyd in the religious world by the pastoral hierarchy. The elect shall reign with Jesus Christ durigatousand years, say the {60} apostolic traditions: that is to say, that during a series of centuie, heintelligence and love of chosen men, devoted to the burden of power, will administer the inerets nd he wealth of the universal family.
At that day, according to the promise of the Gospel, there will be no more than one flock and onepherd.

XVI

THE NUMBER SIXTEEN

SIXTEEN is the number of the temple.
Let us say what the temple of the future will be!
When the spirit of intelligence and love shall have revealed itself, the whole trinity will manifitself in its truth and in its glory.
Humanity, become a queen, and, as it were, risen from the dead, will have the grace of childhood ts poesy, the vigour of youth in its reason, and the wisdom of ripe age in its works.
All those forms, which the divine thought has successively clothed, will be born again, immortal perfect.
All those features which the art of successive nations has sketched will unite themselves, and fohe complete image of God.
Jerusalem will rebuild the Temple of Jehovah on the model prophesied by Ezekiel; and the Christ, and eternal Solomon, will chant, beneath roofs of cedar and of cypress, the Epithalamium of his mrig with holy liberty, the holy bride of the Song of Songs.
But Jehovah will have laid aside his thunderbolts, to bless {61} with both hands the bridegroom ahe bride; he will appear smiling between them, and take pleasure in being called father.
However, the poetry of the East, in its magical souvenirs, will call him still Brahma, and JupiteIndia will teach our enchanted climates the marvellous fables of Vishnu, and we shall place upon h tll bleeding forehead of our well-beloved Christ the triple crown of pearls of the mystical Triuri. Fom that time, Venus, purified under the veil of Mary, will no more weep for her Adonis.
The bridegroom is risen to die no more, and the infernal boar has found death in its momentary viy.
Lift yourselves up again, O Temples of Delphi and of Ephesus! The God of Light and of Art is becthe God of the world, and the Word of God is indeed willing to be called Apollo! Diana will no mr egn widowed in the lonely fields of night; her silvern crescent is now beneath the feet of the rie. But Diana is not conquered by Venus; her Endymion has wakened, and virginity is about to take priin otherhood!
Quit the tomb, O Phidias, and rejoice in the destruction of thy first Jupiter: it is now that tholt conceive a God!

All that was true, all that was beautiful, all that was sweet in the past centuries, will live onore glorified in this transfiguration of the world.
And the beautiful form will remain inseparable from the true idea, as the body will one day be inrable from the soul, when the soul, come to its own power, will have made itself a body in its ow mg.

And bodies and souls, and form and thought, and the whole universe, will be the light, the word, the permanent and visible revelation of God. Amen. So be it. {63}

XVII

THE NUMBER SEVENTEEN

SEVENTEEN is the number of the star; it is that of intelligence and love.
Warrior and bold intelligence, accomplice of divine Prometheus, eldest daughter of Lucifer, hail thee in thine audacity! Thou didst wish to know, and in order to possess, thou didst brave all h hnders, and affronted every abyss!

I believe because I know why and how one must believe; I believe because I love, and fear no more Love! Love! Sublime redeemer and sublime restorer; thou who makest so much happiness, with so mytrtures, thou who didst sacrifice blood and tears, thou who art virtue {64} itself, and the rewadofvitue; force of resignation, belief of obedience, joy of sorrow, life of death, hail! XVIII

THE NUMBER EIGHTEEN

THIS number is that of religious dogma, which is all poetry and all mystery. {65}
The Gospel says that at the death of the Saviour the veil of the Temple was rent, because that demanifested the triumph of devotion, the miracle of charity, the power of God in man, divine humant,ad human divinity, the highest and most sublime of Arcana, the last word of all initiations.
But the Saviour knew that at first men would not understand him, and he said: "You will not be abo bear at present the full light of my doctrine; but, when the Spirit of Truth shall manifest himef e will teach you all truth, and he will cause you to understand the sense of what I have said nt yu.
Now the Spirit of Truth is the spirit of science and intelligence, the spirit of force and of cou.
It is that spirit which solemnly manifested itself in the Roman Church, when it declared in the farticles of its decree of the 12th December, 1845:

1 Degree. --- That if faith is superior to reason, reason ought to endorse the inspirations of fa
2 Degree. --- That faith and science have each their separate domain, and that the one should notrp the functions of the other;
3 Degree. --- That it is proper for faith and grace, not to weaken, but on the contrary to streng and develop reason;
4 Degree. --- That the concourse of reason, which examines, not the decisions of faith, but the nal and rational bases of the authority which decides them, far from injuring faith, can only be ueu o it; in other words, that a faith, perfectly reasonable in its principles, should not fear, bt hold on the contrary, desire the sincere examination of reason.

Such a decree is the accomplishment of a complete religious {66} revolution, it is the inauguratif the reign of the Holy Ghost upon the earth.

XIX

THE NUMBER NINETEEN

IT is the number of light.
It is the existence of God proved by the very idea of God.
Either one must say that Being is the universal tomb where, by an automatic movement, stirs a forr ever dead and corpse-like, or one must admit the absolute principle of intelligence and of life
Is the universal light dead or alive? Is it vowed fatally to the work of destruction, or providelly directed to an immortal birth?
If there be no God, intelligence is only a deception, for it fails to be the absolute, and its idis a lie.
Without God, being is a nothingness affirming itself, life a death in disguise, and light a night ever deceived by the mirage of dreams.
The first and most essential act of faith is then this.
Being exists; and the Being of beings, the Truth of being, is God.
Being is alive with intelligence, and the living intelligence of absolute being is God.
Light is real and life-giving; now, the reality and life of all light is God.
The word of universal reason is an affirmation and not a negation.
How blind are they who do not see that physical light is nothing but the instrument of thought! { Thought alone, then, reveals light, and creates it in using it for its own purposes.
The affirmation of atheism is the dogma of eternal night: the affirmation of God is the dogma of t!

We stop here at the number Nineteen, although the sacred alphabet has twenty-two letters; but thest nineteen are the keys of occult theology. The others are the keys of Nature; we shall return t hmin the third part of this work.

We have given to these bold qabalistic speculations the only form which suits them, that is, poesr the inspiration of the heart.

ARTICLE II

SOLUTION OF THE SECOND PROBLEM

TRUE RELIGION

RELIGION exists in humanity, like love.
Like it, it is unique.
Like it, it either exists, or does not exist, in such and such a soul; but, whether one accepts i denies it, it is in humanity; it is, then, in life, it is in nature itself; it is an incontestabefc of science, and even of reason.
The true religion is that which has always existed, which exists to-day, and will exist for ever.Some one may say that religion is this or that; religion is what it is. This is the true religion n he false religions are superstitions imitated from her, borrowed from her, lying shadows of hesef! One may say of religion what one says of true art. Savage attempts at painting or sculpture are atempts of ignorance to arrive at the truth. Art proves itself by itself, is radiant with its ow pendor, is unique and eternal like beauty.
The true religion is beautiful, and it is by that divine character that it imposes itself on the ect of science, and obtains the assent of reason.
Science dare not affirm or deny those dogmatic hypotheses which are truths for faith; but it mustognize by unmistakable {72} characters the one true religion, that is to say, that which alone meiste name of religion in that it unites all the characters which agree with that great and univeralasirtion of the human soul.
One only thing, which is to all most evidently divine, is manifested in the world.
It is charity.
The work of true religion should be to produce, to preserve, and to spread abroad the spirit of cty.
To arrive at this end she must herself possess all the characteristics of charity, in such a mannhat one could define her satisfactorily, in naming her, "Organic Charity."
Now, what are the characteristics of charity?
It is St. Paul who will tell us.
Charity is patient.
Patient like God, because it is eternal as He is. It suffers persecutions, and never persecutes rs.
It is kindly and loving, calling to itself the little, and not repulsing the great.
It is without jealousy. Of whom, and of what, should it be jealous? Has it not that better part h shall not be taken away from it?
It is neither quarrelsome nor intriguing.
It is without pride, without ambition, without selfishness, without anger.
It never thinks evil, and never triumphs by injustice; for all its joy is comprehended in truth.
It endures everything, without ever tolerating evil.
It believes all; its faith is simple, submissive, hierarchical, and universal. {73}
It sustains all, and never imposes burdens which it is not itself the first to carry.

Religion is patient --- the religion of great thinkers and of martyrs.
It is benevolent like Christ and the apostles, like Vincent de Paul, and like Fenelon.
It envies not either the dignities or the goods of the earth. {74} It is the religion of the fat of the desert, of St. Francis, and of St. Bruno, of the Sisters of Charity, and of the Brothers fSit-Jean-de- Dieu.
It is neither quarrelsome nor intriguing. It prays, does good, and waits.
It is humble, it is sweet-tempered, it inspires only devotion and sacrifice. It has, in short, ahe characteristics of Charity because it is Charity itself.
Men, on the contrary, are impatient, persecutors, jealous, cruel, ambitious, unjust, and they shoemselves as such, even in the name of that religion which they have succeeded in calumniating, bu hc they will never cause to life. Men pass away, but truth is eternal.
Daughter of Charity, and creator of Charity in her own turn, true religion is essentially that whrealizes; she believes in the miracles of faith, because she herself accomplishes them every day hnse practises charity. Now, a religion which practises charity may flatter herself that she reaizs llthe dreams of divine love. Moreover, the faith of the hierarchical church transforms mystiismint relism by the efficacy of her sacraments. No more signs, no more figures whose strength i notin gace,and which do not really give what they promise! Faith animates all, makes all in som sortvisibe andpalpable; even the parables of Jesus Christ take a body and a soul. They show, atJerusaem, th houseof the wicked rich man!! The thin symbolisms of the primitive religions overtuned by cience,and depived of the life of faith, resemble those whitened bones which covered the feld thatEzekiel aw in hi vision. ARTICLE III

SOLUTION OF THE THIRD PROBLEM

THE RATIONALE OF THE MYSTERIES

FAITH being the aspiration to the unknown, the object of faith is absolutely and necessarily this thing --- Mystery.
In order to formulate its aspirations, faith is forced to borrow aspirations and images from the n.
But she specializes the employment of these forms, by placing them together in a manner which, in known order of things, is impossible. Such is the profound reason of the apparent absurdity of smoim.
Let us give an example:
If faith said that God was impersonal, one might conclude that God is only a word, or, at most, ang.
If it is said that God was a person, one would represent to oneself the intelligent infinite, undhe necessarily bounded form of an individual.
It says, "God is one in three persons," in order to express that one conceives in God both unity multiplicity.
The formula of a mystery excludes necessarily the very intelligence of that formula, so far as itborrowed from the world of known things; for, if one understood it, it would express the known an o he unknown.
It would then belong to science, and no longer to religion, that is to say, to faith. {77}
The object of faith is a mathematical problem, whose "x" escapes the procedures of our algebra.
Absolute mathematics prove only the necessity, and, in consequence, the existence of this unknownch we represent by the untranslatable "x."
Now science progresses in vain; its progress is indefinite, but always relatively finite; it willer find in the language of the finite the complete expression of the infinite. Mystery is therefr trnal.
To bring into the logic of the known the terms of a profession of faith is to withdraw them from h, which has for positive bases anti-logic, that is to say, the impossibility of logically explaiigte unknown.
For the Jew, God is separate from humanity; He does not live in His creatures, He is infinite ego
For the Mussulman, God is a word before which one prostrates oneself, on the authority of Mohamme For the Christian, God has revealed himself in humanity, proves Himself by charity, and reigns byrue of the order which constitutes the hierarchy.
The hierarchy is the guardian of dogma, for whose letter and spirit she alike demands respect. Tectarians who, in the name of their reason or, rather, of their individual unreason, have laid had ndogma, have, in the very act, lost the spirit of charity; they have excommunicated themselves. Th Ctholic, that is to say the universal, dogma merits that magnificent name by harmonizing in ne ll he eligious aspirations of the world; with Moses and Mohammed, it affirms the unity of God;withZoroster Hermes and Plato, it recognizes in Him the infinite trinity of its own regeneration;{78} t recncile the living numbers of Pythagoras with the monadic Word of St. John;<<The author hd perhps no pace t continue with a demonstration that the Gospel legend itself is a macedoine of hose ofBacchus Adonis Osiris, and a hundred others, and that the Mass, and Christian ceremonies gnerally,have simlarly paan sources. --- O. M.>> so much, science and reason will agree. It is thn in the yes of reson and o science themselves the most perfect, that is to say the most complete dogma whih has everbeen produed in the world. Let science and reason grant us so much; we shallask nothingmore of the.
"God exists; there is only one God, and He punishes those who do evil," said Moses.
"God is everywhere; He is in us, and the good that we do to me we do it to God," said Jesus.
"Fear" is the conclusion of the dogma of Moses.
"Love" is the conclusion of the dogma of Jesus.
The typical ideal of the life of God in humanity is incarnation.
Incarnation necessitates redemption, and operates it in the name of the reversibility of solidari<This and many similar phrases employed in the controversies of the period are to-day practicallyuitlligible. Levi was at one time a kind of Socialist. --- TRANS.>> or, in other words, of univesa cmmnion, the dogmatic principle of the spirit of charity.
To substitute human arbitrament for the legitimate despotism of the law, to put, in other words, nny in the place of authority, is the work of all Protestantism and of all democracies. What mencl iberty is the sanction of illegitimate authority, or, rather, the fiction of power not sanctioedbyauhority. {79}
John Calvin protested against the stakes of Rome, in order to give himself the right to burn MichServetus. Every people that liberates itself from a Charles I, or a Louis XVI, must undergo a Roepere or a Cromwell and there is a more or less absurd anti-pope being all protestations against heleitmate papacy.
The divinity of Jesus Christ only exists in the Catholic Church, to which He transmits hierarchic His life and His divine powers. This divinity is sacerdotal and royal by virtue of communion; btotide of that communion, every affirmation of the divinity of Jesus Christ is idolatrous, becaus Jsu Crist could not be an isolated God.
The number of Protestants is of no importance to Catholic truth.
If all men were blind, would that be a reason for denying the existence of the sun?
Reason, in protesting against dogma, proves sufficiently that she has not invented it; but she isced to admire the morality which results from that dogma. Now, if morality is a light, it follow htdogma must be a sun; light does not come from shadows.
Between the two abysses of polytheism, and an absurd and ignorant theism, there is only one possimedium: the mystery of the most Holy Trinity.
Between speculative theism, and anthropomorphiosm, there is only one possible medium: the mysteryincarnation.
Between immoral fatality, and Draconic responsibility, which would conclude the damnation of all gs, there is only one possible mean: the mystery of redemption.
The trinity is faith. {80}
The incarnation is hope.
The redemption is charity.
The trinity is the hierarchy.
Incarnation is the divine authority of the Church.
Redemption is the unique, infallible, unfailing and Catholic priesthood.
The Catholic Church alone possesses an invariable dogma, and by its very constitution is incapabl corrupting morality; she does not make innovations, she explains. Thus, for example, the dogma fteimmaculate conception is not new; it was contained in the theotokon of the Council of Ephesus,an te heotokon is a rigorous consequence of the Catholic dogma of the incarnation.
In the same way the Catholic Church makes no excommunications, she declares them; and she alone ceclare them, because she alone is guardian of unity.
Outside the vessel of Peter, there is nothing but the abyss. Protestants are like people who haveown themselves into the water in order to escape sea-sickness.
It is of Catholicity, such as it is constituted in the Roman Church, that one must say what Voltaso boldly said of God: "If it did not exist, it would be necessary to invent it." But if a man hdbe capable of inventing the spirit of charity, he also would have invented God. Charity does no ivet tself, it reveals itself by its works, and it is then that one can cry with the Saviour of he orl: "lessed are the pure in heart, for they shall see God!"
To understand the spirit of charity is to understand all mysteries. {81}



ARTICLE IV

SOLUTION OF THE FOURTH PROBLEM

RELIGION PROVED BY THE OBJECTIONS WHICH PEOPLE
OPPOSE TO IT.

THE objections which one may make against religion may be made either in the name of science, or he name of reason, or in the name of faith.
Science cannot deny the facts of the existence of religion, of its establishment and its influencon the events of history.
It is forbidden to it to touch dogma; dogma belongs wholly to faith.
Science ordinarily arms itself against religion with a series of facts which it is her duty to apiate, which, in fact, she does appreciate thoroughly, but which she condemns still more energeticlytan science does.
In doing that, science admits that religion is right, and herself wrong; she lacks logic, manifeshe disorder which every angry passion introduces into the spirit of man, and admits the need thati a of being ceaselessly redressed and directed by the spirit of charity.
Reason, on its side, examines dogma and finds it absurd.
But, if it were not so, reason would understand it; if reason understood it, it would no longer be formula of the unknown. {82}
It would be a mathematical demonstration of the infinite.
It would be the infinite finite, the unknown known, the immeasurable measured, the indicible name That is to say that dogma could only cease to be absurd in the eyes of reason to become, in the e f faith, science, reason and good sense in one, the most monstrous and the most impossible of al burities.
Remain the objections of dissent.
The Jews, our fathers in religion, reproach us with having attacked the unity of God, with havingnged the immutable and eternal law, with adoring the creature instead of the Creator.
These heavy reproaches are founded on their perfectly false notion of Christianity.
Our God is the God of Moses, unique, immaterial, infinite God, sole object of worship, and ever tame.
Like the Jews, we believe Him to be present everywhere, but, as they ought to do, we believe Him ng, thinking and loving in humanity, and we adore Him in His works.
We have not changed His law, for the Jewish Decalogue is also the law of Christians.
The law is immutable because it is founded on the eternal principles of Nature; but the worship nsitated by the needs of man may change, and modify itself, parallel with the changes in men themsle. This signifies that the worship itself is immutable, but modifies itself as language does.
Worship is a form of instruction; it is a language; one must translate it when nations no longer rstand it. {83}
We have translated, and not destroyed, the worship of Moses and of the prophets.
In adoring God in creation, we do not adore the creation itself.
In adoring God in Jesus Christ, it is God alone whom we adore, but God united to humanity.
In making humanity divine, Christianity has revealed the human divinity.
The God of the Jews was inhuman, because they did not understand Him in His works.
We are, then, more Israelite than the Israelites themselves. What they believe, we believe with , and better than they do. They accuse us of having separated ourselves from them, and, on the cnrr, it is they who wish to separate from us.
We wait for them, the heart and the arms wide open.
We are, as they are, the disciples of Moses.
Like them, we come from Egypt, and we detest its slavery. But we have entered into the Promised , and they obstinately abide and die in the desert.
Mohammedans are the bastards of Israel, or rather, they are his disinherited brothers, like Esau.Their belief is illogical, for they admit that Jesus is a great prophet, and they treat Christian sifidels.
They recognize the Divine inspiration of Moses, yet they do not look upon the Jews as their broth
They believe blindly in their blind prophet, the fatalist Mohammed, the enemy of progress and of rty.
Nevertheless, do not let us take away from Mohammed the {84} glory of having proclaimed the unityGod among the idolatrous Arabs.
There are pure and sublime pages in the Qur'an.
In reading those pages, one may say with the children of Ishmael, "There is no other God but God, Mohammed is his prophet."
There are three thrones in heaven for the three prophets of the nations; but, at the end of time,ammed will be replaced by Elias.
The Mussulmans do not reproach the Christians; they insult them.
They call them infidels and "giaours," that is to say, dogs. We have nothing to reply to them.
One must not refute the Turks and the Arabs; one must instruct and civilize them.
Remain dissident Christians, that is to say, those who, having broken the bond of unity, declare selves strangers to the charity of the Church.
Greek orthodoxy, that twin of the Roman Church which has not grown greater since its separation, h counts no longer in religion, which, since Photius, has not inspired a single eloquence, is a cuc ecome entirely temporal, whose priesthood is no more than a function regulated by the imperialpoic o the Tsar of All the Russias; a curious mummy of the primitive Church, still coloured and glde wih al its legends and all its rites, which its popes no longer understand; the shadow of a lvingchurh, bt one which insisted on stopping when that church moved on, and which is now no more han is bloted-ot and headless silhouette.
Then, the Protestants, those eternal regulators of anarchy, {85} who have broken down dogma, and trying always to fill the void with reasonings, like the sieve of the Danaides; these weavers of eiius fantasy, all of whose innovations are negative, who have formulated for their own use an unnon aling itself better known, mysteries better explained, a more defined infinite, a more restranedimmnsiy, a more doubting faith, those who have quintessentialized the absurd, divided charity,and akenactsof anarchy for the principles of an entirely impossible hierarchy; those men who wishto relize alvaton by faith alone, because charity escapes them, and who can no longer realize it,even uon theearth,for their pretended sacraments are no longer anything but allegorical mummeries they n longergive grce; they no longer make God seen and touched; they are no longer, in a word,the sign of the lmighty ower of faith, but the compelled witnesses of the eternal impotence of dobt.
It is, then, against faith itself that the Reformation protested! Protestants were right only in r protest against the inconsiderate and persecuting zeal which wished to force consciences. Theycamd the right to doubt, the right to have less religion than others, or even to have none at all teyhae shed their blood for that sad privilege; they conquered it, they possess it; but they wil no tae aay from us that of pitying them and loving them. When the need to believe again takes tem, hen heirheart revolts against the tyranny of a falsified reason when they become tired of theemptyabstrction of their arbitrary dogma, of the vague observances of their ineffective worship; hen thir comunion ithout the real presence, their churches without divinity, and their morality wthout gace finlly frihten {86} them; when they are sick with the nostalgia of God --- will they nt rise u like th prodiga son, and come to throw themselves at the feet of the successor of Peter,saying: "ather, wehave sinnd against heaven and in thy sight, and we are no more worthy to be caled thy son, but coun us among he humblest of thy servants"?
We will not speak of the criticism of Voltaire. That great mind was dominated by an ardent love ruth and justice, but he lacked that rectitude of heart which the intelligence of faith gives. Vlar could not admit faith, because he did not know how to love. The spirit of charity did not reea isef to that soul which had no tenderness, and he bitterly criticized the hearth of which he dd nt fel he warmth, and the lamp of which he did not see the light. If religion were such as he aw i, hewoul have been a thousand times right to attack it, and one would be obliged to fall on oe's kees bfore he heroism of his courage. Voltaire would be the Messiah of good sense, the Herculs destuctor f fanaicism. ... But he laughed too much to understand Him who said: "Happy are they ho weep" and te philoophy of laughter will never have anything in common with the religion of teas.
Voltaire parodied the Bible, dogma and worship; and then he mocked and insulted that parody.
Only those who recognize religion in Voltaire's parody can take offence at it. The Voltaireans aike the frogs in the fable who leap upon the log, and then make fun of royal majesty. They are a iety to take the log for a king, they are at liberty to make once more that Roman caricature of hih erullian once made mirth, that which represented the {87} God of the Christians under the figre f amanwith an ass's head. Christians will shrug their shoulders when they see this knavery, ad pry Go forthe poor ignorants who imagine that they insult them.
M. the Count Joseph de Maistre, after having, in one of his most eloquent paradoxes, represented hangman as a sacred being, and a permanent incarnation of divine justice upon earth, suggested thtoeshould raise to the old man of Ferney a statue executed by the hangman. There is depth in thisthugt. Voltaire, in effect, also was, in the world, a being at the same time providential and fatl, ndoed ith insensibility for the accomplishment of his terrible functions. He was, in the doman ofinteligece, a hangman, an extirminator armed by the justice of God Himself.
God sent Voltaire between the century of Bossuet and that of Napoleon in order to destroy everyththat separates those two geniuses and to unite them in one alone.
He was the Samson of the spirit, always ready to shake the columns of the temple; but in order toe him turn in spite of himself the mill of religious progress, Providence made him blind of heart

ARTICLE V

SOLUTION OF THE LAST PROBLEM

TO SEPARATE RELIGION FROM SUPERSTITION AND FANATICISM

SUPERSTITION, from the Latin word "superstes," surviving, is the sign which survives the idea whit represents; it is the form preferred to the thing, the rite without reason, faith become insenst hough isolating itself. It is in consequence the corpse of religion, the death of life, stupefctonsustituted for inspiration.
Fanaticism is superstition become passionate, its name comes from the word "fanum," which signifitemple," it is the temple put in place of God, it is the human and temporal interest of the pries usituted for the honour of priesthood, the wretched passion of the man exploiting the faith of te elevr.
In the fable of the ass loaded with relics, La Fontaine tells us that the animal thought that he being adored; he did not tell us that certain people indeed thought that they were adoring the anml These people were the superstitious.
If any one had laughed at their stupidity, he would very likely have been assassinated, for from rstition to fanaticism is only one step.
Superstition is religion interpreted by stupidity; fanaticism is religion serving as a pretext toy.
Those who intentionally and maliciously confound religion {89} itself with superstition and fanatm, borrow from stupidity its blind prejudices, and would borrow perhaps in the same way from fanaiimits injustices and angers.
Inquisitors or Septembrisors,<<Those who took part in the massacres of the Revolution of the 4th ember, 1792. --- TRANS.>> what matter names? The religion of Jesus Christ condemns, and has alway odmned, assassins.

RESUME OF THE FIRST PART

IN THE FORM OF A DIALOGUE

FAITH, SCIENCE, REASON.

SCIENCE. You will never make me believe in the existence of God.
FAITH. You have not the privilege of believing, but you will never prove to me that God does notst.
SCIENCE. In order to prove it to you, I must first know what God is.
FAITH. You will never know it. If you knew it, you could teach it to me; and when I knew it, I ld no longer believe it.
SCIENCE. Do you then believe without knowing what you believe?
FAITH. Oh, do not let us play with words! It is you who do not know what I believe, and I beliet precisely because you do not know it. Do you pretend to be infinite? Are you not stopped at eeysep by mystery? Mystery is for you an infinite ignorance which would reduce to nothing your finteknwldge, if I did not illumine it with my burning aspirations; and if, when you say, "I no longr kow, I id not cry, "As for me, I begin to believe."
SCIENCE. But your aspirations and their object are not (and cannot be for me) anything but hypots. {91}
FAITH. Doubtless, but they are certainties for me, since without those hypotheses I should be doul even about your certainties.
SCIENCE. But if you begin where I stop, you begin always too rashly and too soon. My progress b witness that I am ever advancing.
FAITH. What does your progress matter, if I am always walking in front of you?
SCIENCE. You, walking! Dreamer of eternity, you have disdained earth too much; your feet are beed.
FAITH. I make my children carry me.
SCIENCE. They are the blind carrying the blind; beware of precipices!
FAITH. No, my children are by no means blind; on the contrary, they enjoy twofold sight: they sey thine eyes, what thou canst show them upon earth, and they contemplate, by mine, what I show thmi eaven.
SCIENCE. What does Reason think of it?
REASON. I think, my dear teachers, that you illustrate a touching fable, that of the blind man ahe paralytic. Science reproaches Faith with not knowing how to walk upon the earth, and Faith sasta Science sees nothing of her aspirations and of eternity in the sky. Instead of quarrelling, cinc ad Faith ought to unite; let Science carry Faith, and let Faith console Science by teaching er o hpe nd to love!
SCIENCE. It is a fine ideal, but Utopian. Faith will tell me absurdities. I prefer to walk wit her.
FAITH. What do you call absurdities?
SCIENCE. I call absurdities propositions contrary to my demonstrations; as, for example, that thmake one, that a {92} God has become man, that is to say, that the Infinite has made itself finit,ta the Eternal died, that God punished his innocent Son for the sin of guilty men. ...
FAITH. Say no more about it. As enunciated by you, these propositions are in fact absurdities. you know what is the number of God, you who do not know God? Can you reason about the operationso h unknown? Can you understand the mysteries of charity? I must always be absurd for you; for,ifyo uderstood them, my affirmations would be absorbed by your theorems; I should be you, and youwoud b me or, to put it better, I should no longer exist, and Reason, in the presence of the infiite,woul hal, blinded for evermore by your doubts, which are as infinite as space.
SCIENCE. At least, you should never usurp my authority, or give me the lie in my own domains.
FAITH. I have never done so, and I could never do so.
SCIENCE. So! You have never believed, for example, that a virgin could become a mother, withoutsing to be a virgin, in the physical, natural, positive order of things, in spite of all the lawso aure; you do not affirm that a piece of bread is not only a God, but a real human body with itsboesan its veins, its organs, its blood; such, in short, that you make of your children who eat tis rea a ittle race of cannibals.
FAITH. There is not a single Christian who would not be revolted by what you have just said. Thufficiently proves that they do not understand my teachings in this positive and gross sense. Th uenatural which I affirm is above Nature, and consequently could never oppose itself to her; thewodsofFaith are understood only by Faith; Science has only to repeat them to pervert their propersene. I eploy these {93} words because I have no others; but since you find my remarks absurd, yo shold cnclue from that that I give to the same words a meaning which escapes you. When the Saviur reealedthe dgma of the real presence, did he not say: "The flesh profiteth nothing, but my wors are pirit nd lif." I do not give you the mystery of the incarnation for an anatomical phenomenn, nor hat of ransubsantiation for a chemical manipulation. By what right do you exclaim "Absurd"? I donot reasn about ny of the things you know; by what right do you say that I talk unreasonaly?
SCIENCE. I begin to understand you, or rather, I see that I shall never understand you. This beso, let us remain separate; I shall never have need of you.
FAITH. I am less proud, and I recognize that you may be useful to me perhaps. Perhaps, too, youl be very sad and very desperate without me, and I will not leave you unless Reason consents.
REASON. Be well 'ware of doing so! I am necessary to both of you. And I, what should I do withyou? In order to be just, I need to know and to believe. But I must never confound what I know ihwat I believe. To know is to believe no longer; to believe is not to know as yet. The object f cinc is the known; Faith does not occupy itself with that, and leaves it all to Science. The ojec ofFaih is the unknown; Science may seek it, but not define it; she is then obliged, at least roviionaly, o accept the definitions of Faith which it is impossible for her even to criticize. nly, f Scince rnounces Faith, she renounces hope and love, whose existence and necessity are as eident or Scince asfor Faith. Faith, as a psychological fact, pertains to the realm of {94} Scienc; and Sience, s the mnifestation of the light of God within the human intelligence, pertains to te realm f Faith. Scienceand Faith must then admit each other, respect each other mutually, suppor each othr, and ber each oter aid in case of need, but without ever encroaching the one upon the ther. Themeans of uiting themis --- never to confound them. Never can there be contradiction beween them, or althoughthey use th same words,, they do not speak the same language.
FAITH. Oh, well, Sister Science; what do you say about it?
SCIENCE. I say that we are separated by a deplorable misunderstanding, and that henceforward we l be able to walk together. But to which of your different creeds do you wish to attach me? ShalIb Jewish, Catholic, Mohammedan, or Protestant?
FAITH. You will remain Science, and you will be universal.
SCIENCE. That is to say, Catholic, if I understand you correctly. But what should I think of thfferent religions?
FAITH. Judge them by their works. Seek true Charity, and when you have found her, ask her to whreligion she belongs.
SCIENCE. It is certainly not to that of the Inquisition, and of the authors of the Massacre of Sartholomew.
FAITH. It is to that of St. John the Almoner, of St. Francois de Sales,<<Levi was certainly nevee dupe of this boudoir Theologian. He accepted him without perusal, as the Englishman accepts Shksere and Milton. --- O. M.>> of St. Vincent de Paul, of Fenelon, and so many more. {95}

M.>> when one pescts in the name of Him who commands us to forgive our enemies, when one propagates darkness in te am o Him who tells us not to hide the light under a bushel, is it just to attribute the crime t th vey lw which condemns it? Say, if you wish to be just, that in spite of religion, much evil as ben dne uon earth. But also, to how many virtues has it not given birth? How many are the deotion, howmany he sacrifices, of which we do not know! Have you counted those noble hearts, bothmen an women who rnounced all joys to enter the service of all sorrows? Those souls devoted to lbour an to praer, whohave strewn their pathways with good deeds? Who founded asylums for orphansand old en, hosptals forthe sick, retreats for the repentant? The heretics, moreover, wre themselvs murderers Have you orgotten the burning of Michael Servetus and the massacre of ourpriests, renwed, still i the name ofhumanity and reason, by the revolutionaries who hated the Inqisition and te Massacre ofSt. Bartholomw? Men are always cruel, it is true, but only when they frget the religon whose watchords are blessng and pardon.
SCIENCE. O Faith! Pardon me, then, if I cannot believe; {96} but I know now why you believe. Ipect your hopes, and share your desires. But I must find by seeking; and in order to seek, I mustdut
REASON. Work, then, and seek, O Science, but respect the oracles of Faith! When your doubt leav gap in universal enlightenment, allow Faith to fill it! Walk distinguished the one from the othr u leaning the one upon the other, and you will never go astray.

PART II

PHILOSOPHICAL MYSTERIES

PRELIMINARY CONSIDERATIONS

IT has been said that beauty is the splendour of truth.
Now moral beauty is goodness. It is beautiful to be good.
To be intelligently good, one must be just.
To be just, one must act reasonably.
To act reasonably, one must have the knowledge of reality.
To have the knowledge of reality, one must have consciousness of truth.
To have consciousness of truth, one must have an exact notion of being.
Being, truth, reason and justice are the common objects of the researches of science, and of the rations of faith. The conceptions, whether real or hypothetical, of a supreme power transform jutc nto Providence; and the notion of divinity, from this point of view, becomes accessible to scinc hrslf.
Science studies Being in its partial manifestation; faith supposes it, or rather admits it "a pri as a whole.
Science seeks the truth in everything; faith refers everything to an universal and absolute truth Science records realities in detail: faith explains them by {98} totalized reality to which scien anot bear witness, but which the very existence of the details seems to force her to recognize adtoadit.
Science submits the reasons of persons and things to the universal mathematical reason; faith seeor rather supposes, an intelligent and absolute reason for (and above) mathematics themselves.
Science demonstrates justice by justness; faith gives an absolute justness to justice, in subording it to Providence.
One sees here all that faith borrows from science, and all that science, in its turn, owes to fai Without faith, science is circumscribed by an absolute doubt, and finds itself eternally penned wn the risky empiricism of a reasoning scepticism; without science, faith constructs its hypothese trndom, and can only blindly prejudge the causes of the effects of which she is ignorant.
The great chain which reunites science and faith is analogy.
Science is obliged to respect a belief whose hypotheses are analogous to demonstrated truths. Fa which attributes everything to God, is obliged to admit science as being a natural revelation whc,b the partial manifestation of the laws of eternal reason, gives a scale of proportion to all te spraions and to all the excursions of the soul into the domain of the unknown.
It is, then, faith alone that can give a solution to the mysteries of science; and in return, it cience alone that demonstrates the necessity of the mysteries of faith.
Outside the union and the concourse of these two living forces of the intelligence, there is for nce nothing but {99} scepticism and despair, for faith nothing but rashness and fanaticism.
If faith insults science, she blasphemes; if science misunderstand faith, she abdicates.
Now let us hear them speak in harmony!
"Being is everywhere," says science. "it is multiple and variable in its forms, unique in its ess, and immutable in its laws. The relative demonstrates the existence of the absolute. Intelligec xsts in being. Intelligence animates and modifies matter."
"Intelligence is everywhere," says faith; "Life is nowhere fatal because it is ruled. This rule he expression of supreme Wisdom. The absolute in intelligence, the supreme regulator of forms, telvng ideal of spirits, is God."
"In its identity with the ideal, being is truth," says science.
"In its identity with the ideal, truth is God," replies faith.
"In its identity with my demonstrations, being is reality," says science.
"In its identity with my legitimate aspirations, reality is my dogma," says faith.
"In its identity with the Word, being is reason," says science.
"In its identity with the spirit of charity, the highest reason is my obedience," says faith.
"In its identity with the motive of reasonable acts, being is justice," says science.
"In its identity with the principle of charity, justice is Providence," replies faith.
Sublime harmony of all certainties with all hopes, of the {100} absolute in intelligence with theolute in love! The Holy Spirit, the spirit of charity, should then conciliate all, and transformalito His own light. Is it not the spirit of intelligence, the spirit of science, the spirit of ouse, he spirit of force? "He must come," says the Catholic liturgy, "and it will be, as it were a ew reaion; and He will change the face of the earth."
"To laugh at philosophy is already to philosophize," said Pascal, referring to that sceptical andredulous philosophy which does not recognize faith. And if there existed a faith which trampled cec underfoot, we should not say that to laugh at such a faith would be a true act of religion, fr elgin, which is all charity, does not tolerate mockery; but one would be right in blaming this oveforignrance, and in saying to this rash faith, "Since you slight your sister, you are not the augher o God"
Truth, reality, reason, justice, Providence, these are the five rays of the flamboyant star in thntre of which science will write the word "being," --- to which faith will add the ineffable nameo o.



SOLUTION OF THE PHILOSOPHICAL
PROBLEMS

FIRST SERIES

QUESTION. What is truth?
ANSWER. Idea identical with being. {101}<<WEH NOTE: Obvious error: confusion of map with territo>
Q. What is reality?
A. Knowledge identical with being.
Q. What is reason?
A. The Word identical with being.
Q. What is justice?
A. The motive of acts identical with being.
Q. What is the absolute?
A. Being.
Q. Can one conceive anything superior to being?
A. No; but one conceives in being itself something supereminent and transcendental.
Q. What is that?
A. The supreme reason of being.
Q. Do you know it, and can you define it?
A. Faith alone affirms it, and names it God.
Q. Is there anything above truth?
A. Above known truth, there is unknown truth.
Q. How can one construct reasonable hypotheses with regard to this truth?
A. By analogy and proportion.
Q. How can one define it?
A. By the symbols of faith.
Q. Can one say of reality the same thing as of truth?
A. Exactly the same thing.
Q. Is there anything above reason?
A. Above finite reason, there is infinite reason.
Q. What is infinite reason?
A. It is that supreme reason of being that faith calls God.<<WEH NOTE: This is the characteristirase of the philosophy of 18th century enlightenment: "God is Reason" --- also the characteristicerr 19th century philosophy continued this into Determinism and the now discredited concept of "atra Lw".>>
Q. Is there anything above justice? {102}
A. Yes; according to faith, there is the Providence of God, and the sacrifice of man.
Q. What is this sacrifice?
A. It is the willing and spontaneous surrender of right.
Q. Is this sacrifice reasonable?
A. No; it is a kind of folly greater than reason, for reason is forced to admire it.
Q. How does one call a man who acts according to truth, reality, reason and justice?
A. A moral man.
Q. And if he sacrifices his interests to justice?
A. A man of honour.
Q. And if in order to imitate the grandeur and goodness of Providence he does more than his dutyd sacrifices his right to the good of others?
A. A hero.
Q. What is the principle of true heroism?
A. Faith.
Q. What is its support?
A. Hope.
Q. And its rule?
A. Charity.
Q. What is the Good?
A. Order.
Q. What is the Evil?
A. Disorder.
Q. What is permissible pleasure?
A. Enjoyment of order.
Q. What is forbidden pleasure?
A. Enjoyment of disorder. {103}
Q. What are the consequences of each?
A. Moral life and moral death.
Q. Has then hell, with all its horrors, its justification in religious dogma?
A. Yes; it is a rigorous consequence of a principle.
Q. What is this principle?
A. Liberty.
Q. What is liberty?
A. The right to do one's duty, with the possibility of not doing it.
Q. What is failing in one's duty?
A. It involves the loss of one's right. Now, right being eternal, to lose it is to suffer an etl loss.
Q. Can one repair a fault?
A. Yes; by expiation.
Q. What is expiation?
A. Working overtime. Thus, because I was lazy yesterday, I had to do a double task to-day.
Q. What are we to think of those who impose on themselves voluntary sufferings?
A. If they do so in order to overcome the brutal fascination of pleasure, they are wise; if to sr instead of others, they are generous; but if they do it without discretion and without measure,te re imprudent.
Q. Thus, in the eyes of true philosophy, religion is wise in all that it ordains?
A. You see that it is so.
Q. But if, after all, we were deceived in our eternal hopes?
A. Faith does not admit that doubt. But philosophy herself should reply that all the pleasures he earth are not {104} worth one day of wisdom, and that all the triumphs of ambition are not worhasngle minute of heroism and of charity.

SECOND SERIES

QUESTION. What is man?
ANSWER. Man is an intelligent and corporeal being made in the image of God and of the world, oneessence, triple in substance, mortal and immortal.
Q. You say, "triple in substance." Has man, then, two souls or two bodies?
A. No; there is in him a spiritual soul, a material body, and a plastic medium.
Q. What is the substance of this medium?
A. Light, partially volatile, and partially fixed.
Q. What is the volatile part of this light?
A. Magnetic fluid.<<WEH NOTE: This passage derives from the efforts of Newton, Mesmer and othersquantify the astral body. 18th and 19th century efforts to measure ectoplasm, oddic force, etc. n ophysically measure an essence of life have persisted to the verge of the 21st century in a strng peuo-science. Q. But will science preoccupy herself with it?

Q. Give us some notions of this plastic medium.
A. It is formed of astral or terrestrial light, and transmits {105} the double magnetization of o the human body. The soul, by acting on this light through its volitions, can dissolve it or coglt it, project it or withdraw it. It is the mirror of the imagination and of dreams. It reactsupn henervous system, and thus produces the movements of the body. This light can dilate itself ndeiniely and communicate its reflections at considerable distances; it magnetizes the bodies subitte to he ation of man, and can, by concentrating itself, again draw them to him. Q. What is animal magnetism?
A. The action of one plastic medium upon another, in order to dissolve or coagulate it. By augmng the elasticity of the vital light and its force of projection, one sends it forth as far as on il and withdraws it completely loaded with images; but this operation must be favoured by the slmbr f he subject, which one produces by coagulating still further the fixed part of his medium.
Q. Is magnetism contrary to morality and religion?
A. Yes, when one abuses it.
Q. In what does the abuse of it consist?
A. In employing it in a disordered manner, or for a disordered object.
Q. What is a disordered magnetism? {106}
A. An unwholesome fluidic emission, made with a bad intention; for example, to know the secrets thers, or to arrive at unworthy ends.
Q. What is the result of it?
A. It puts out of order the fluidic instrument of precision, both in the case of the magnetizer of the magnetized. To this cause one must attribute the immoralities and the follies with which ra number of those who occupy themselves with magnetism are reproached.
Q. What conditions are required in order to magnetize properly?
A. Health of spirit and body; right intention, and discreet practice.
Q. What advantageous results can one obtain by discreet magnetism?
A. The cure of nervous diseases, the analysis of presentiments, the re- establishment of fluidicmonies, and the rediscovery of certain secrets of Nature.
Q. Explain that to us in a more complete manner.
A. We shall do so in the third part of this work, which will treat specially of the mysteries ofure.

Whence one may deduce these corollaries:
Regularity and variety in movement result from the different combinations of equilibrium.
A point equilibrated on all sides remains at rest, for the very reason that it is endowed with mo.

To direct the magnetic forces is then to destroy or create forms; to produce to all appearance, o destroy bodies; it is to exercise the almighty power of Nature.
