CHAPTER II

LIVE AND DEATH. --- SLEEP AND WAKING

SLEEP is an incomplete death; death is a complete sleep.
Nature subjects us to sleep in order to accustom us to the idea of death, and warns us by dreams he persistence of another life.
The astral light into which sleep plunges us is like an ocean in which innumerable images are afl flotsam of wrecked existences, mirages and reflections of those which pass, presentiments of thoewih are about to be.
Our nervous disposition attracts to us those images which correspond to our agitation, to the natof our fatigue, just as a magnet, moved among particles of various metals, would attract to itsel n hoose particularly the iron filings. {117}
Dreams reveal to us the sickness or the health, the calm or the disturbance, of our plastic mediund consequently, also, that of our nervous apparatus.
They formulate our presentiments by the analogy which the images bear to them.
For all ideas have a double significance for us, relating to our double life.
There exists a language of sleep; in the waking state it is impossible to understand it, or even rder its words.
The language of slumber is that of nature, hieroglyphic in its character, and rhythmical in its ss.
Slumber may be either giddy or lucid.
Madness is a permanent state of vertiginous somnambulism.
A violent disturbance may wake madmen to sense, or kill them.
Hallucinations, when they obtain the adhesion of the intelligence, are transitory attacks of madn
Every mental fatigue provokes slumber; but if the fatigue is accompanied by nervous irritation, tlumber may be incomplete, and take on the character of somnambulism.
One sometimes goes to sleep without knowing it in the midst of real life; and then instead of thig, one dreams.
How is it that we remember things which have never happened to us? Because we dreamt them when wiwake.
This phenomenon of involuntary and unperceived sleep when it suddenly traverses real life, often ens to those who over-excite their nervous organism by excesses either of work, vigil, drink, or rtim. {118}
Monomaniacs are asleep when they perform unreasonable acts. They no longer remember anything on ng.
When Papvoine was arrested by the police, he calmly said to them these remarkable words: "You areing the other for me."
It was the somnambulist who was still speaking.
Edgar Poe, that unhappy man of genius who used to intoxicate himself, has terribly described the ambulism of monomaniacs. Sometimes it is an assassin who hears, and who thinks that everybody her,trough the wall of the tomb, the beating of his victim's heart; sometimes it is a poisoner who,bydit f saying to himself, "I am safe, provided I do not go and denounce myself," ends by dreamin alud hathe is denouncing himself, and in fact does so. Edgar Poe himself invented neither the prson northe acts of these strange novels; he dreamt them waking, and that is why he clothed them o wel withall te colours of a shocking reality.
Dr. Briere de Boismont in his remarkable work on "Hallucinations," tells the story of an Englishmtherwise quite sane, who thought that he had met a stranger and made his acquaintance, who took hmt unch at his tavern, and then having asked him to visit St. Paul's in his company, had tried tothowhi from the top of the tower which they had climbed together.<<WEH NOTE: Crowley elaborated sverl o thse anecdotes into his stories.>>
From that moment the Englishman was obsessed by this stranger, whom he alone could see, and whom lways met when he was alone, and had dined well.
Precipices attract; drunkenness calls to drunkenness; madness has invincible charms for madness. n a man {119} succumbs to sleep, he holds in horror everything which might wake him. It is the sm ih the hallucinated, with statical somnambulists, maniacs, epileptics, and all those who abando temeles to the delirium of a passion. They have heard the fatal music, they have entered into te dnceof eath; and they feel themselves dragged away into the whirl of vertigo. You speak to the, thy nomorehear you; you warn them, they no longer understand you, but your voice annoys them; tey ar aslep wit the sleep of death.
Death is a current which carries you away, a whirlpool which draws you down, but from the bottom hich the least movement may make you climb again. The force or repulsion being equal to that of trcion, at the very moment of expiring, one often attaches oneself again violent to life. In our "Dogme et rituel de la haute magie," we had the temerity to say that the resurrection of tead is not an impossible phenomenon even on the physical plane; and in saying that, we have not dne r in any way contradicted the fatal law of death. We have been told as an actual fact a story whose authenticity we will not altogether guarantee.
We are about to repeat it because it may be true.
Certain persons who doubted both religion and magnetism, of that incredulous class which is ready all superstitions and all fanaticisms, had persuaded a poor girl to submit to their experiments o ee. This girl was of an impressionable and nervous nature, fatigued moreover by the excesses f lfewhich had been more than irregular, while she was already disgusted with existence. They pt hr t slep; bade her see; she weeps and struggles. They speak to her of God; she trembles in evry lmb.
"No," said she, "no;" He frightens me; I will not look at Him."
"Look at Him, I wish it."
She opens her eyes, her pupils expand; she is terrifying.
"What do you see?"
"I should not know how to say it. ... Oh for pity's sake awaken me!" {124}
"No, look, and say what you see."
"I see a black night in which whirl sparks of every colour around two great ever-rolling eyes. Fthese eyes leap rays whose spiral whorls fill space. ... Ho, it hurts me! Wake me!"
"No, look."
"Where do you wish me to look now?"
"Look into Paradise."
"No, I cannot climb there; the great night pushes me back, I always fall back."
"Very well then, look into hell."
Here the sleep-waker became convulsively agitated.
"No, no!" she cried sobbing; "I will not! I shall be giddy; I should fall! Oh, hold me back! Hme back!"
"No, descend."
"Where do you want me to descend?"
"Into hell."
"But it is horrible! No! No! I will not go there!"
"Go there."
"Mercy!"
"Go there. It is my will."
The features of the sleep-waker become terrible to behold; her hair stands on end; her wide-openees show only the white; her breast heaves, and a sort of death-rattle escapes from her throat.
"Go there. It is my will," repeats the magnetizer.
"I am there!" says the unhappy girl between her teeth, falling back exhausted. Then she no longeswers; her head hangs heavy on her shoulder; her arms fall idly by her side. They approach her. Te ouch her. They try to {125} waken her, but it is too late; the crime was accomplished; the woanwa dad. It was to the public incredulity in the matter of magnetism that the authors of this scriegius xperiment owed their own immunity from prosecution. The authorities held an inquest, an deah wa attibuted to the rupture of an aneurism. The body, anyhow, bore no trace of violence; tey ha it bried,and there was an end of the matter.
Here is another anecdote which we heard from a travelling companion.
Two friends were staying in the same inn, and sharing the same room. One of them had a habit of tng in his sleep, and, at that time, would answer the questions which his comrade put to him. Onengt he suddenly uttered stifled cries; his companion woke up and asked him what was the matter.
"But, don't you see," said the sleeper, "don't you see that enormous stone ... it is becoming loorom the mountain ... it is falling on me, it is going to crush me."
"Oh, well, get out of its way!"
"Impossible! My feet are caught in brambles that cling ever closer. Ah! Help! Help! There is great stone coming right upon me!"
"Well, there it is!" said the other laughing, throwing the pillow at his head in order to wake hi A terrible cry, suddenly strangled in his throat, a convulsion, a sigh, then nothing more. CHAPTER III

MYSTERIES OF HALLUCINATIONS AND OF THE EVOCATION OF SPIRITS

AN hallucination is an illusion produced by an irregular movement of the astral light.
It is, as we said previously, the admixture of the phenomena of sleep with those of waking.
Our plastic medium breathes in and out the astral light or vital soul of the earth, as our body bhes in and out the terrestrial atmosphere. Now, just as in certain places the air is impure and ntftfor breathing, in the same way, certain unusual circumstances may make the astral light unwholsoe,an not assimilable.
The air of some places may be too bracing for some people, and suit others perfectly; it is exacthe same with the magnetic light.
The plastic medium is like a metallic statue always in a state of fusion. If the mould is defect it becomes deformed; if the mould breaks, it runs out.
The mould of the plastic medium is balanced and polarized vital force. Our body, by means of the ous system, attracts and retains this fugitive form of light; but local fatigue, or partial over-xieent of the apparatus, may occasion fluidic deformities.
These deformities partially falsify the mirror of the imagination, and thus occasion habitual halnations to the static type of visionary.
The plastic medium, made in the image and likeness of our {127} body, of which it figures every o in light, has a sight, touch, hearing, smell and taste which are proper to itself; it may, when ti ver-excited, communicate them by vibrations to the nervous apparatus in such a manner that thehaluintion is complete. The imagination seems then to triumph over Nature itself, and produces tulystrngephenomena. The material body, deluged with fluid, seems to participate in the fluidic qalites, t esapes from the operation of the laws of gravity, becomes momentarily invulnerable, andeven nvisile, i a circle of persons suffering from collective hallucination. The convulsionariesof St.Medard as on knows, had their flesh torn off with red-hot pincers, had themselves felled lie oxen,and grond likecorn, and crucified, without suffering any pain; they were levitated, walkedabout hed downwads, and te bent pins and digested them.

The vulgar are always deceived about magic, and confuse adepts with enchanters. True magic, thatto say, the traditional science of the magi, is the mortal enemy of enchantment; it prevents, or ae o cease, sham miracles, hostile to the light, that fascinate a small number of prejudiced or cedlos itnesses. "This first signature is hieratical, and bears reference to the occult characters of the divine w.

"The glorification of tyranny and of revolt.
"The hieroglyphic sign of the unclean rite, with which, rightly or wrongly, the Templars were rephed; it is the sign of disorder and of eternal despair."

The sign of the lingam borne upon the forehead is in India the distinguishing mark of the worship of Shiva the destroyer; for that sign being that of the great magical arcanum, which refers to temsery of universal generation, to bear it on the forehead is to make profession of dogmatic shamlesnss "Now," say the Orientals, "the day when there is no longer modesty in the world, the worl, gvenove to debauch which is sterile, will end at once for lack of mothers. Modesty is the accetanc of aterity."
The hand with the three large fingers closed expresses the negation of the ternary, and the affiron of the natural forces alone.
The ancient hierophants, as our learned and witty friend Desbarolles is about to explain in an adble book which is at present in the press, had given a complete "resume" of magical science in th ua hand. It is already a long time since this doctrine, or, rather, this antidoctrine, began to work upon world, to plunge it into universal anarchy. But the law of equilibrium will save us, and alreadytegeat movement of reaction has begun.

One day a workman paid a visit to Eliphas Levi. He was a tall man of some fifty years old, of frappearance, and speaking in a very reasonable manner. Questioned as to the motive of his visit, erpied: "You ought to know it well enough; I am come to beg and pray you to return to me what I hvelot.

One word upon this little book so much decried.
The "grimoire" of Honorius is composed of an apocryphal constitution of Honorius II, for the evocn and control of spirits; then of some superstitious receipts ... it was the manual of the bad pret ho practised Black Magic during the darkest periods of the middle ages. Strange as may be the facts which we have just related, there remains for us to unveil a tragic d much more extraordinary still.
It refers to the deed of blood which at the beginning of this year plunged Paris and all Christeninto mourning and stupefaction; a deed in which no one suspected that Black Magic had any part.
Here is what happened:

But in order to communicate with the phantom of absolute evil, to realize that phantom to the poif seeing and touching it, is it not necessary to be without conscience and without judgment?
There is doubtless the secret of this incredible perversity, of this murderous fury, of this unwhome hate against all order, all ministry, all hierarchy, of this fury, above all, against the dogawih sanctifies peace, obedience, gentleness, purity, under so touching an emblem as that of a mohe.
This wretch thought himself sure not to die. The Emperor, thought he, would be obliged to pardon; an honourable exile awaited him; his crime would give him an enormous celebrity; his reveries wudb bought for their weight in gold by the booksellers. He would become immensely rich, attract henoic of a great lady, and marry beyond the seas. It is by such promises that the phantom of th deil,lon ago, lured Gilles de Laval, Seigneur of Retz, and made him wade from crime to crime. But the aberrations of perversity do not constitute an insanity; the execution of this wretch proit. {177}
One knows what desperate resistance he made to his executioners. "It is treason," said he; "I ca die so! Only one hour, an hour to write to the Emperor! The Emperor is bound to save me."
Who, then, was betraying him?
Who, then, had promised him life?
Who, then, had assured him beforehand of a clemency which was impossible, because it would revolt conscience of the public?
Ask all that of the "grimoire" of Honorius!
Two incidents in this tragic story bear upon the phenomena produced by Mr. Home: the noise of therm heard by the wicked priest in his early evocations, and the difficulty which he found in expresn is real thought in the presence of Eliphas Levi.
One may also comment upon the apparition of the sinister man taking pleasure in the public grief, uttering an indeed infernal word in the midst of the consternation of the crowd, an apparition ol oiced by the ecstatic of La Salette, the too celebrated Mlle. de La Merliere, who has the air ate al f a worthy individual, but very excitable, and perhaps capable of acting and speaking withot kowig i herself, under the influence of a sort of ascetic sleep-waking.
This word "sleep-waking" brings us back to Mr. Home, and our anecdotes have not made us forget whhe title of this work promised to our readers.
We ought, then, to tell them what Mr. Home is.
We keep our promise.
"Mr. Home is an invalid suffering from a contagious sleep-waking." {178}
This is an assertion.
It remains to us to give an explanation and a demonstration.
That explanation and demonstration, in order to be complete, demand a work sufficient to fill a b
That book has been written, and we shall publish it shortly.
Here is the title:
"The Reason of Miracles, or the Devil at the Tribunal of Science."<<That was the title which we ided at that time to give to the book which we now publish. --- E. L.>>
"Why the devil?"
Because we have demonstrated by facts what Mr. de Mirville had, before us, incompletely set forth We say "incompletely"; because the devil is, for Mr. de Mirville, a fantastic personage, while fou,it is the misuse of a natural force.
A medium once said: "Hell is not a place, it is a state."
We shall be able to add: "The devil is not a person or a force; it is a vice, and in consequence,eakness."
Let us return for a moment to the study of phenomena!
Mediums are, in general, of poor health and narrow limitations.
They can accomplish nothing extraordinary in the presence of calm and educated persons.
One must be accustomed to them before seeing or feeling anything.
The phenomena are not identical for all present. For example, where one will see a hand, anotherl perceive nothing but a whitish smoke. {179}
Persons impressed by the magnetism of Mr. Home feel a sort of indisposition; it seems to them thae room turns round, and the temperature seems to them to grow rapidly lower.
The miracles are more successful in the presence of a few people chosen by the medium himself.
In a meeting of several persons, it may be that all will see the miracles --- with the exception ne, who will see absolutely nothing.
Among the persons who do see, all do not see the same thing.
Thus, for example:
One evening, at Mme. de V------'s, the medium made appear a child which that lady had lost. Mme.B------ alone saw the child; Count de M------ saw a little whitish vapour, in the shape of a pyrai;te others saw nothing.
Everybody knows that certain substances, hashish, for example, intoxicate without taking away the of reason, and cause to be seen with an astonishing vividness things which do not exist.
A great part of the phenomena of Mr. Home belong to a natural influence similar to that of hashis This is the reason why the medium refuses to operate except before a small number of persons chosb himself.
The rest of these phenomena should be attributed to magnetic power.
To see anything at Mr. Home's "seances" is not a reassuring index of the health of him who sees.
And even if his health should be in other ways excellent, {180} the vision indicates a transitoryturbation of the nervous apparatus in its relation to imagination and light.
If this perturbation were frequently repeated, he would become seriously ill.
Who knows how many collapses, attacks of tetanus, insanities, violent deaths, the mania of table-ing has already produced?
These phenomena become particularly terrible when perversity takes possession of them.
It is then that one can really affirm the intervention and the presence of the spirit of evil.
Perversity or fatality, these pretended miracles obey one of these two powers.
As to qabalistic writings and mysterious signatures, we shall say that they reproduce themselves he magnetic intuition of the mirages of thought in the universal vital fluid.
These instinctive reflections may be produced if the magic Word has nothing arbitrary in it, and he signs of the occult sanctuary are the natural expressions of absolute ideas.
It is this which we shall demonstrate in our book.
But, in order not to send back our readers from the unknown to the future, we shall detach before two chapters of that unpublished work, one upon the qabalistic Word, the other upon the secrets fteQabalah, and we shall draw conclusions which will compete in a manner satisfactory to all the xpantin which we have promised in the matter of Mr. Home.

Light creates forms in accordance with the laws of eternal mathematics, by the universal equilibrof light and shadow.
The primitive signs of thought trace themselves by themselves in the light, which is the materialtrument of thought.
God is the soul of light. The universal and infinite light is for us, as it were, the body of go The Qabalah, or transcendental magic, is the science of light.
Light corresponds to life.
The kingdom of shadows is death.
All the dogmas of true religion are written in the Qabalah in characters of light upon a page of ow.
The page of shadows consists of blind beliefs.
Light is the great plastic medium.
The alliance of the soul and the body is a marriage of light and shadow.
Light is the instrument of the Word, it is the white writing of God upon the great book of night.Light is the source of thought, and it is in it that one must seek for the origin of all religiou om. But there is only one true dogma, as there is only one pure light; shadow alone is infinitey ared
Light, shadow, and their harmony, which is the vision of beings, form the principle analogous to great dogmas of Trinity, of Incarnation, and of Redemption.
Such is also the mystery of the cross.

There is a fundamental and universal letter which generates all the others. It is the IOD.

Each letter represents a number: each assemblage of letters, a series of numbers. {184}

The letters are shorthand hieroglyphs.

THE MOTHERS

The IOD. --- The absolute principle, the productive being.
The MEM. --- Spirit, or the Jakin of Solomon.
The SCHIN. --- Matter, or the column called Boaz.

THE DOUBLE LETTERS

BETH. Reflection, thought, the moon, the Angel Gabriel, Prince of mysteries.
GIMEL. Love, will, Venus, the Angel Anael, Prince of life and death.
DALETH. Force, power, Jupiter, Sachiel, Melech, King of kings.
KAPH. Violence, strife, work, Mars, Samael Zebaoth, Prince of Phalanges.
PE. Eloquence, intelligence, Mercury, Raphael, Prince of sciences.
RESH. Destruction and regeneration, Time, Saturn, Cassiel, King of tombs and of solitude.
TAU. Truth, light, the Sun, Michael, King of the Elohim. {185}

The idea of a perfect and immutable order in nature, the notion of an ascending hierarchy and of scending influence in all beings, had furnished to the ancient hierophants the first classificatino he whole of natural history. Minerals, vegetables, animals were studied analogically; and the atrbued their origin and their properties to the passive or to the active principle, to the darkessor o te light. Such is the origin of tat fablous ntural istory, in which lions allow themselves to be defeated by cocks, where dolphinsdie of orrow fr the igratitude of men, in which mandrakes speak, and the stars sing. The other fables of the sham natural history of the ancet re explained in the same manner, and in this allegorical use of analogies, one can {189} alreay ndrsand the possible abuses and predict the errors to which the Qabalah was obliged to give birh.
The law of analogies, in fact, has been for qabalists of a secondary rank the object of a blind aanatical faith. It is to this belief that one must attribute all the superstitions with which th dps of occult science have been reproached. This is how they reasoned:
The sign expresses the thing.
The thing is the virtue of the sign.
There is an analogical correspondence between the sign and the thing signified.
The more perfect is the sign, the more entire is the correspondence.
To say a word is to evoke a thought and make it present. To name God is to manifest God.
The word acts upon souls, and souls react upon bodies; consequently one can frighten, console, cato fall ill, cure, even kill, and raise from the dead by means of words.
To utter a name is to create or evoke a being.
In the name is contained the "verbal" or spiritual doctrine of the being itself.
When the soul evokes a thought, the sign of that thought is written automatically in the light.
To invoke is to adjure, that is to say, to swear by a name; it is to perform an act of faith in tname, and to communicate in the virtue which it represents.
Words in themselves are, then, good or evil, poisonous or wholesome.
The most dangerous words are vain and lightly uttered words, because they are the voluntary abort of thought. {190}
A useless word is a crime against the spirit of intelligence; it is an intellectual infanticide.
Things are for every one what he makes of them by naming them. The "word" of every one is an impion or an habitual prayer.
To speak well is to live well.
A fine style is an aureole of holiness.
From these principles, some true, others hypothetical, and from the more or less exaggerated consnces that they draw from them, there resulted for superstitious qabalists and absolute confidencei nhantments, evocations, conjurations and mysterious prayers. Now, as faith has always accomplihe mrales, apparitions, oracles, mysterious cures, sudden and strange maladies, have never been lckig t it

The hand is the instrument of action in man: it is, like the face, a sort of synthesis of the ner system, and should also have features and physiognomy. The habitual folds of the hand determine its lines. These lines are, then, the traces of habits, the patient observer will know how to recognize them and how to judge them. The man whose hand od adly is clumsy or unhappy. The hand has three principal functions: to grasp, to hold, and to {11 hnde. Now here is what Paracelsus reserved for initiates alone, {213} and what we have understood throueciphering the qabalistic characters, and the allegories of which he makes use in his work:
The human soul is material; the divine "mens" is offered to it to immortalize it and to make it lspiritually and individually, but its natural substance is fluidic and collective.
There are, then, in man, two lives: the individual or reasonable life, and the common or instinctlife. It is by this latter that one can live in the bodies of others, since the universal soul, fwih each nervous organism has a separate consciousness, is the same for all.
We live in a common and universal life in the embryonic state, in ecstasy, and in sleep. In sleen fact, reason does not act, and logic, when it mingles in our dreams, only does so by chance, inacrance with the accidents of purely physical reminiscences.
In dreams, we have the consciousness of the universal life; we mingle ourselves with water, fire,, and earth; we fly like birds; we climb like squirrels; we crawl like serpents; we are intoxicatdwt astral light; we plunge into the common reservoir, as happens in a more complete manner in deth bt hen (and it is thus that Paracelsus explains the mysteries of the other life) the wicked, tat s t sa, those who have allowed themselves to be dominated by the instinct of the brute to the rejuice f huan reason, are drowned in the ocean of the common life with all the anguish of eterna deat; theother swim upon it, and enjoy for ever the riches of that fluid gold which they have suceededin domnating
This identity of all physical life permits the stronger {214} souls to possess themselves of the tence of the others, and to make auxiliaries of them; it explains sympathetic currents either nea rdstant, and gives the whole secret of occult medicine, because the principle of this medicine i te rad hypothesis of universal analogies, and, attributing all the phenomena of physical life totheuniersl agent, teaches that one must act upon the astral body in order to react upon the materal vsibl bod; it teaches also that the essence of the astral light is a double movement of attracion ad replsion just as human bodies attract and repel one another, they can also absorb themselvs, extnd oneinto aother, and make exchanges; the ideas or imaginations of one can influence the frm of te other and susequently react upon the exterior body.

Let us now come to the secret of the Great Work, which we have given only in Hebrew, without voweints, in the "Rituel de la haute magie." Here is the complete text in Latin, as one finds in on ae14 of the Sepher Yetzirah, commented by the alchemist Abraham (Amsterdam, 1642): {216}

SEMITA XXXI

Vocatur intelligentia perpetua; et quare vocatur ita? Eo quod ducit motum solis et lunae juxta citutionem eorum; utrumque in orbe sibi conveniente.

Rabbi Abraham F.'. D.'. dicit:

Semita trigesima prima vocatur intelligentia perpetua: et illa ducit solem et lunam et reliquas sas et figuras, unum quodque in orbe suo, et impertit omnibus creatis juxta dispositionem ad signae iuras.

Here is the French translation of the Hebrew text which we have transcribed in our ritual:

"The thirty-first path is called the perpetual intelligence; and it governs the sun and the moon, the other stars and figures, each in its respective orb. And it distributes what is needful to l rated things, according to their disposition to the signs and figures."

This text, one sees, is still perfectly obscure for whoever is not acquainted with the characteri value of each of the thirty-two paths. The thirty-two paths are the ten numbers and the twenty-w iroglyphic letters of the Qabalah. The thirty-first refers to HB:Shin , which represents the mgi lmp or the light between the horns of Baphomet. It is the qabalistic sign of the OD, or astra liht,wit its two poles, and its balanced centre. One knows that in the language of the alchemis thesun igniies gold, the moon silver, and that the other stars or planets refer to the other metls. ne {27} shuld now be able to understand the thought of the Jew Abraham.
The secret fire of the masters of alchemy was, then, electricity; and there is the better half ofir grand arcanum; but they knew how to equilibrate its force by a magnetic influence which they cnetated in their athanor. This is what results from the obscure dogmas of Basil Valentine, of Berar Tevsan, and of Henry Khunrath, who, all of them, pretended to have worked the transmutation, lke aymnd ully, like Arnaud de Villeneuve, and like NIcholas Flamel.
The universal light, when it magnetizes the worlds, is called astral light; when it forms the met one calls it azoth, or philosophical mercury; when it gives life to animals, it should be calledaia magnetism.
The brute is subject to the fatalities of this light; man is able to direct it.
It is the intelligence which, by adapting the sign to the thought, creates forms and images.
The universal light is like the divine imagination, and this world, which changes ceaselessly, yeer remaining the same with regard to the laws of its configuration, is the vast dream of God.
Man formulates the light by his imagination; he attracts to himself the light in sufficient quants to give suitable forms to his thoughts and even to his dreams; if this light overcomes him, if edons his understanding in the forms which he evokes, he is mad. But the fluidic atmosphere of mdmn s ften a poison for tottering reason and for exalted imaginations.
The forms which the over-excited imagination produces {218} in order to lead astray the understan, are as real as photographic images. One could not see what does not exist. The phantoms of dras nd even the dreams of the waking, are then real images which exist in the light.
There exist, besides these, contagious hallucinations. But we here affirm something more than orry hallucinations.
If the images attracted by diseased brains are in some sense real, can they not throw them withouemselves, as real as they relieve them?
These images projected by the complete nervous organism of the medium, can they not affect the coe organism of those who, voluntarily or not, are in nervous sympathy with the medium?
The things accomplished by Mr. Home prove that all this is possible.
Now, let us reply to those who think that they see in these phenomena manifestations of the otherld and facts of necromancy.
We shall borrow our answer from the sacred book of the qabalists, and in this our doctrine is tha the rabbis who compiled the Zohar.

AXIOM

The spirit clothes itself to descend, and strips itself to rise.
In fact:
Why are created spirits clothed with bodies?
It is that they must be limited in order to have a possible existence. Stripped of all body, and me consequently {219} without limit, created spirits would lose themselves in the infinite, and fo ak of the power to concentrate themselves somewhere, they would be dead and impotent everywhere lstasthey would be in the immensity of God.
All created spirits have, then, bodies, some subtler, some grosser, according to the surroundingswhich they are called to live.
The soul of a dead man would, then, not be able to live in the atmosphere of the living, any moren we can live in earth or in water.
For an airy, or rather an ethereal, spirit, it would be necessary to have an artificial body simito the apparatus of our divers, in order that it might come to us.
All that we can see of the dead are the reflections which they have left in the atmospheric lightght whose imprints we evoke by the sympathy of our memories.
The souls of the dead are above our atmosphere. Our respirable air becomes earth for them. Thiswhat the Saviour declares in His Gospel, when He makes the soul of a saint say:
"Now the great abyss is established between us, and those who are above can no longer descend to e who are below."

One touches them as one sees them; half illusion, half magnetic and nervous force.
These, it seems to us, are very precise and very clear explanations.
Let us reason a little with those who support the theory of apparitions from another world:
Either those hands are real bodies, or they are illusions.
If they are bodies, they are, then, not spirits.
If they are illusions produced by mirages, either in us, or outside ourselves, you admit my argum
Now, one remark!
It is that all those who suffer from luminous congestion or contagious somnambulism, perish by a ent or, at least, a sudden death.
It is for this reason that one used to attribute to the devil the power of strangling sorcerers.
The excellent and worthy Lavater habitually evoked the alleged spirit of Gablidone.
He was assassinated.
A lemonade-seller of Leipzig, Schroepfer, evoked the animated images of the dead. He blew out hiains with a pistol.
One knows what was the unhappy end of Cagliostro.
A misfortune greater than death itself is the only thing that can save the life of these imprudenperimenters.
They may become idiots or madmen, and then they do not die, if one watches over them with care tovent them from committing suicide.
Magnetic maladies are the road to madness; they are {221} always born from the hypertrophy or atr of the nervous system.
They resemble hysteria, which is one of their varieties, and are often produced either by excessescelibacy, or those or exactly the opposite kind.
One knows how closely connected with the brain are the organs charged by Nature with the accomplent of her noblest work: those whose object is the reproduction of being.
One does not violate with impunity the sanctuary of Nature.
Without risking his own life, no one lifts the veil of the great Isis.
Nature is chaste, and it is to chastity that she gives the key of life.
To give oneself up to impure loves is to plight one's troth to death.
Liberty, which is the life of the soul, is only preserved in the order of Nature. Every voluntarsorder wounds it, prolonged excess murders it.
Then, instead of being guided and preserved by reason, one is abandoned to the fatalities of the and flow of magnetic light.
The magnetic light devours ceaselessly, because it is always creating, and because, in order to pce continually, one must absorb eternally.
Thence come homicidal manias and temptations to commit suicide.
Thence comes that spirit of perversity which Edgar Poe has described in so impressive and accuratmanner, and which Mr. de Mirville would be right to call the devil. {222}
The devil is the giddiness of the intelligence stupefied by the irresolution of the heart.
It is a monomania of nothingness, the lure of the abyss; independently of what it may be accordin the decisions of the Catholic, Apostolic, and Roman faith, which we have not the temerity to touh

CHAPTER IV

FLUIDIC PHANTOMS AND THEIR MYSTERIES

THE ancients gave different names to these: larvae, lemures (empuses). They loved the vapour of sblood, and fled from the blade of the sword.
Theurgy evoked them, and the Qabalah recognized them under the name of elementary spirits.
They were not spirits, however, for they were mortal.
They were fluidic coagulations which one could destroy by dividing them.
There were a sort of animated mirages, imperfect emanations of human life. The traditions of Blacgic say that they were born owing to the celibacy of Adam. Paracelsus says that the vapours of tebod of hysterical women people the air with phantoms; and these ideas are so ancient, that {226}wefid races of them in Hesiod, who expressly forbids that linen, stained by a pollution of any sot, houd b dried before a fire.
Persons who are obsessed by phantoms are usually exalted by too rigorous celibacy, or weakened byesses.
Fluidic phantoms are the abortions of the vital light; they are plastic media without body and wit spirit, born from the excesses of the spirit and the disorders of the body.
These wandering media may be attracted by certain degenerates who are fatally sympathetic to themd who lend them at their own cost a factitious existence of a more or less durable kind. They thnsre as supplementary instruments to the instinctive volitions of these degenerates: never to cur tem aways to send them farther astray, and to hallucinate them more and more.

BOOK II

MAGICAL MYSTERIES

CHAPTER I

THEORY OF THE WILL

HUMAN life and its innumerable difficulties have for object, in the ordination of eternal wisdom, education of the will of man.
The dignity of man consists in doing what he will, and in willing the good, in conformity with thowledge of truth.
The good in conformity with the true, is the just.
Justice is the practice of reason.
Reason is the work of reality.
Reality is the science of truth.
Truth is idea identical with being.
Man arrives at the absolute idea of being by two roads, experience and hypothesis.
Hypothesis is probable when it is necessitated by the teachings of experience; it is improbable osurd when it is rejected by this teaching.
Experience is science, and hypothesis is faith.
True science necessarily admits faith; true faith necessarily reckons with science.
Pascal blasphemed against science, when he said that by reason man could not arrive at the knowleof any truth. {234}
In fact, Pascal died mad.
But Voltaire blasphemed no less against science, when he declare that every hypothesis of faith wbsurd, and admitted for the rule of reason only the witness of the senses.
Moreover, the last word of Voltaire was this contradictory formula: "GOD AND LIBERTY."
God! that is to say, a Supreme Master, excludes every idea of liberty, as the school of Voltaire rstood it.
And Liberty, by which is meant an absolute independence of any master, which excludes all idea of.
The word GOD expresses the supreme personification of law, and by consequence, of duty; and if by word LIBERTY, you are willing to accept our interpretation, THE RIGHT OF DOING ONE'S DUTY, we inortrn will take it for a motto, and we shall repeat, without contradiction and without error: "GO AD IBRTY."
As there is no liberty for man but in the order which results from the true and the good, one may that the conquest of liberty is the great work of the human soul. Man, by freeing himself from i vl passions and their slavery, creates himself, as it were, a second time. Nature made him livngan sffering; he makes himself happy and immortal; he thus becomes the representative of divinit upn erth and (relatively) exercises its almighty power.

AXIOM I
Nothing resists the will of man, when he knows the truth, and wills the good. {235}

AXIOM II
To will evil, is to will death. A perverse will is a beginning of suicide.

AXIOM III
To will good with violence, is to will evil, for violence produces disorder, and disorder produceil.

AXIOM IV
One can, and one should, accept evil as the means of good; but one must never will it or do it, owise one would destroy with one hand what one builds with the other. Good faith never justifies a ens; it corrects them when one undergoes them, and condemns them when one takes them.

AXIOM V
To have the right to possess always, one must will patiently and long.

AXIOM VI
To pass one's life in willing that it is impossible to possess always, is to abdicate life and ac the eternity of death.

AXIOM VII
The more obstacles the will surmounts, the stronger it is. It is for this reason that Christ gloed poverty and sorrow.

AXIOM VIII
When the will is vowed to the absurd, it is reproved by eternal reason.

AXIOM IX
The will of the just man is the will of God himself, and the law of Nature. {236}

AXIOM X
It is by the will that the intelligence sees. If the will is healthy, the sight is just. God sa"Let there be light!" and light is; the will says, "Let the world be as I will to see it!" and th neligence sees it as the will has willed. This is the meaning of the word, "So be it,"<<WEH NOT: ides "Amen".>> which confirms acts of faith.

AXIOM XI
When one creates phantoms for oneself, one puts vampires into the world, and one must nourish thehildren of a voluntary nightmare with one's blood, one's life, one's intelligence, and one's reasn ihout ever satisfying them.

AXIOM XII
To affirm and to will what ought to be is to create; to affirm and will what ought not to be, is estroy.

AXIOM XIII
Light<<Meaning again the special "light" spoken of previously. --- TRANS.>> is an electric fire py Nature at the service of the will; it lights those who know how to use it, it burns those who auei.

AXIOM XIV
The empire of the world is the empire of the light.<<Meaning again the special "light" spoken of iously. --- TRANS.>>

AXIOM XV
Great intellects whose wills are badly balanced are like comets which are aborted suns.

AXIOM XVI
To do nothing is as fatal as to do evil, but it is more cowardly. The most unpardonable of mortans is inertia. {237}

AXIOM XVII
To suffer is to work. A great sorrow suffered is a progress accomplished. Those who suffer much more than those who do not suffer.

AXIOM XVIII
Voluntary death from devotion is not suicide; it is the apotheosis of the will.

AXIOM XIX
Fear is nothing but idleness of the will, and for that reason public opinion scourges cowards.

AXIOM XX
Succeed in not fearing the lion, and the lion will fear you. Say to sorrow: "I will that you be easure, more even than a pleasure, a happiness."

AXIOM XXI
A chain of iron is easier to break than a chain of flowers.

AXIOM XXII
Before saying that a man is happy or unhappy, find out what the direction of his will has made of: Tiberius died every day at Capri, while Jesus proved his immortality and even his divinity on Clayand upon the Cross.

CHAPTER II

THE POWER OF THE WORD

It is the word which creates forms; and forms in their turn react upon the word, in order to modit and complete it.
Every word of truth is a beginning of an act of justice.
One asks if man may sometimes be necessarily driven to evil. Yes, when his judgment is false, annsequently his word unjust.
But one is responsible for a false judgment as for a bad action.
What falsifies the judgment is selfishness and its unjust vanities.
The unjust word, unable to realize itself by creation, realizes itself by destruction. It must er slay or be slain.
If it were able to remain without action, it would be the greatest of all disorders, an abiding bhemy against truth.
Such is that idle word of which Christ has said that one will give account at the Day of Judgment jesting word, a comicality which "recreates" and causes laughter, is not an idle word.
The beauty of the word is a splendour of truth. A true word in always beautiful, a beautiful wor always true.
For this reason works of art are always holy when they are beautiful. {239}
What does it matter to me that Anacreon should sing of Bathyllus, if in his verse I hear the note that divine harmony which is the eternal hymn of beauty? Poetry is pure as the Sun: it spreads t el of light over the errors of humanity. Woe to him who would lift the veil in order to perceie higsugly!
The Council of Trent decided that it was permissible for wise and prudent persons to read the boof the ancients, even those which were obscene, on account of the beauty of the form. A statue ofNr r of Heliogabalus made like a masterpiece of Phidias, would it not be an absolutely beautiful ndaboltely good work? --- and would not he deserve the execration of the whole world who would prpos tobrek it because it was the representation of a monster?
Scandalous statues are those which are badly sculptured, and the Venus of Milo would be desecrate one placed her beside some of the Virgins which they dare to exhibit in certain churches.
One realizes evil in books of morality ill-written far more than in the poetry of Catullus or theenious Allegories of Apuleius.
There are no bad books, except those which are badly conceived and badly executed.
Every word of beauty is a word of truth. It is a light crystallized in speech.
But in order that the most brilliant light may be produced and made visible, a shadow is necessarnd the creative word, that it may become efficacious, needs contradictions. It must submit to th rel of negation, of sarcasm, and then to that more cruel yet, of indifference and forgetfulness. {40 Te Master said: "If a corn of wheat fall into the ground and die, it abideth alone; but if i di, i brngeth forth much fruit."
Affirmation and negation must, then, marry each other, and from their union will be born the pracl truth, the real and progressive word. It is necessity which should constrain the workmen to choefr the corner-stone that which they had at first despised and rejected. Let contradiction, the, evr iscourage men of initiative! Earth is necessary for the ploughshare, and the earth resistsbecuseit s in labour. It defends itself like all virgins; it conceives and brings forth slowly lke al mohers You, then, who wish to sow a new plant in the field of intelligence, understand andrespet themodesies and reluctances of limited experience and slow-moving reason.
When a new word comes into the world, it needs swaddling clothes and bandages; genius brought it h, but it is for experience to nourish it. Do not fear that it will die of neglect! Oblivion isfri a favourable time of rest, and contradictions help it to grow. When a sun bursts forth in spceitcrates worlds or attracts them to itself. A single spark of fixed light promises a universe o sace
All magic is in a word, and that word pronounced qabalistically is stronger than all the powers oaven, Earth and Hell. In order to pronounce duly the great words of the Qabalah, {241} one must pronounce them with a cete intelligence, with a will that nothing checks, an activity that nothing daunts. In magic, tohv aid is to have done; the word begins with letters, it ends with acts. One does not really wil athngunless one wills it with all one's heart, to the point of breaking for it one's dearest affctins;andwith all one's forces, to the point of risking one's health, one's fortune, and one's lie.
It is by absolute devotion that faith proves itself and constitutes itself. But the man armed witch a faith will be able to move mountains.
The most fatal enemy of our souls is idleness. Inertia intoxicates us and sends us to sleep; but sleep of inertia is corruption and death. The faculties of the human soul are like the waves ofteoean. To keep them sweet, they need the salt and bitterness of tears: they need the whirlwindsofHeve: they need to be shaken by the storm.
When, instead of marching upon the path of progress, we wish to have ourselves carried, we are slng in the arms of death. It is to us that it is spoken, as to the paralytic man in the Gospel, "aeu thy bed and walk!" It is for us to carry death away, to plunge it into life.

FOURTH PART

THE GREAT PRACTICAL SECRETS OR THE REALIZATION
OF SCIENCE

INTRODUCTION

THE lofty sciences of the Qabalah and of Magic promise man an exceptional, real, effective, effic power, and one should regard them as false and vain if they do not give it.
Judge the teachers by their works, said the supreme Master. This rule of judgment is infallible.If you wish me to believe in what you know, show me what you do.
God, in order to exalt man to moral emancipation, hides Himself from him and abandons to him, aft fashion, the government of the world. He leaves Himself to be guessed by the grandeurs and harmne f nature, so that man may progressively make himself perfect by ever exalting the idea that hemaesfo himself of its author.
Man knows God only by the names which he gives to that Being of beings, and does not distinguish but by the images of Him which he endeavours to trace. He is then in a manner the creator of HimWohs created him. He believes himself the mirror of God, and by indefinitely enlarging his own mrae,hethinks that he may be able to sketch in infinite space the shadow of Him Who is without bod, wthot sadow, and without space. {267}
TO CREATE GOD, TO CREATE ONE'S SELF, TO MAKE ONE'S SELF INDEPENDENT, IMMORTAL AND WITHOUT SUFFERIthere certainly is a programme more daring than the dream of Prometheus. Its expression is bold otepoint of impiety, its thought ambitious to the point of madness. Well, this programme is onlypaadxial in its form, which lends itself to a false and sacrilegious interpretation. In one sens itis erfctly reasonable, and the science of the adepts promises to realize it, and to accomplishit i perectin.
Man, in effect, creates for himself a God corresponding to his own intelligence and his own goodn he cannot raise his ideal higher than his moral development permits him to do. The God whom he drsis always an enlargement of his own reflection. To conceive the absolute of goodness and justceistobe one's self exceeding just and good.
The moral qualities of the spirit are riches, and the greatest of all riches. One must acquire tby strife and toil. One may bring this objection, the inequality of aptitudes; some children arebr ith organisms nearer to perfection. But we ought to believe that such organisms result from amoe dvnced work of Nature, and the children who are endowed with them have acquired them, if not y teirownefforts, at least by the consolidated works of the human beings to whom their existence s bond. It i a secret of Nature, and Nature does nothing by chance; the possession of more develoed inellecual fculties, like that of money and land, constitutes an indefeasible right of transmision ad inheitance
Yes, man is called to complete the work of his creator, and every instant employed by him to imprhimself or to {268} destroy himself, is decisive for all eternity. It is by the conquest of an itlience eternally clear and of a will eternally just, that he constitutes himself as living for eerallie, since nothing survives injustice and error but the penalty of their disorder. To undersandgoo isto will it, and on the plane of justice to will is to do. For this reason the Gospel tels u tha menwill be judged according to their works.
Our works make us so much what we are, that our body itself, as we have said, receives the modifion, and sometimes the complete change, of its form from our habits.
A form conquered, or submitted to, becomes a providence, or a fatality, for all one's existence. se strange figures which the Egyptians gave to the human symbols of divinity represent the fatal om. Typhon has a crocodile's head. He is condemned to eat ceaselessly in order to fill his hippootmu blly. Thus he is devoted, by his greed and his ugliness, to eternal destruction.
Man can kill or vivify his faculties by negligence or by abuse. He can create for himself new faies by the good use of those which he has received from Nature. People often say that the affectoswll not be commanded, that faith is not possible for all, that one does not re-make one's own carctr. All these assertions are true only for the idle or the perverse. One can make one's self aitful pius, loving, devoted, when one wishes sincerely to be so. One can give to one's spirit te cam ofjustess, as to one's will the almighty power of justice. Once can reign in Heaven by virue offaith on erth by virtue of science. The man who knows how to command himself is king of allNature {269
We are going to state forthwith, in this last book, by what means the true initiates have made thlves the masters of life, how they have overcome sorrow and death; how they work upon themselves n ters the transformation of Proteus; how they exercise the divining power of Apollonius; how the mketh gold of Raymond Lully and of Flamel; how in order to renew their youth they possess the seret ofPosel the Re-arisen, and those alleged to have been in the keeping of Cagliostro. In short we re ging o speak the last word of magic.

Everyone knows the fable of Circe and understands its allegory. {270}
The fatal ascendant of one person on another is the true wand of Circe.
One knows that almost all human physiognomies bear a resemblance to one animal or another, that i say, the "signature" of a specialized instinct.
Now, instincts are balanced by contrary instincts, and dominated by instincts stronger than those In order to dominate sheep, the dog plays upon their fear of wolves.
If you are a dog, and you want a pretty little cat to love you, you have only one means to take: etamorphose yourself into a cat.
But how! By observation, imitation and imagination. We think that our figurative language will nderstood for once, and we recommend this revelation to all who wish to magnetize: it is the deeps fall the secrets of their art.
Here is the formula in technical terms:
"To polarize one's own animal light, in equilibrated antagonism with the contrary pole."
Or:
To concentrate in one's self the special qualities of absorption in order to direct their rays tos an absorbing focus, and vice versa.
This government of our magnetic polarization may be done by the assistance of the animal forms ofch we have spoken; they will serve to fix the imagination.
Let us give an example:
You wish to act magnetically upon a person polarized like yourself, which, if you are a magnetizeou will divine at the first contact: only that person is a little less strong that you {271} are,amue, while you are a rat. Make yourself a cat, and you will capture it.

Animals are the living symbols of the instincts and passions of men. If you make a man timid, yoange him into a hare. If, on the contrary, you drive him to ferocity, you make a tiger of him.

CHAPTER III

THE GRAND ARCANUM OF DEATH

WE often become sad in thinking that the most beautiful life must finish, and the approach of therible unknown that one calls death disgusts us with all the joys of existence.
Why be born, if one must live so little? Why bring up {281} with so much care children who must Such is the question of human ignorance in its most frequent and its saddest doubts.
This, too, is what the human embryo may vaguely ask itself at the approach of that birth which isut to throw it into an unknown world by stripping it of its protective envelope. Let us study th ytry of birth, and we shall have the key of the great arcanum of death!

Then comes an hour ofanguish andconvulsion;its bonds beak; it feels that it is about to fall into the gulf of the unknwn. It is acomplished; t falls, it s crushed with pain, a strange cold seizes it, it breathes a ast sigh whic turns into afirst cry; itis dead to embryonic life, it is born to human life!
During embryonic life it seemed to it that the placenta was its body, and it was in fact its specembryonic body, a body useless for another life, a body which had to be thrown off as an unclean hn t the moment of birth.
The body of our human life is like a second envelope, useless for the third life, and for that re we throw it aside at the moment of our second birth.
Human life compared to heavenly life is veritably an embryo. When our evil passions kill us, Natmiscarries, and we are born before our time for eternity, which exposes us to that terrible dissouinwhich St. John calls the second death.
According to the constant tradition of ecstatics, the abortions of human life remain swimming in terrestrial atmosphere which they are unable to surmount, and which {283} little by little absorb hmand drowns them. They have human form, but always lopped and imperfect; one lacks a hand, anohe a am, this one is nothing but a torso, and that is a pale rolling head. They have been prevened romrisng to heaven by a wound received during human life, a moral wound which has caused a phyicaldefomity and through this wound, little by little, all of their existence leaks away.
Soon their moral soul will be naked, and in order to hide its shame by making itself at all costsew veil, it will be obliged to drag itself into the outer darkness, and pass slowly through the da e, the slumbering waters of ancient chaos. These wounded souls are the larvae of the second fomaio o the embryo; they nourish their airy bodies with a vapour of shed blood, and they fear the oin ofthesword. Frequently they attach themselves to vicious men and live upon their lives, as te emryo ivesin its mother's womb. In these circumstances, they are able to take the most horribl form to rpreset the frenzied desires of those who nourish them, and it is these which appear undr the iguresof demns to the wretched operators of the nameless works of black magic.
These larvae fear the light, above all the light of the mind. A flash of intelligence is sufficito destroy them as by a thunderbolt, and hurl them into that Dead Sea which one must not confuse ihte sea in Palestine so-called. All that we reveal in this place belongs to the tradition of sers ndcan only stand before science in the name of that exceptional philosophy, which Paracelsus alld te pilosophy of sagacity, "philosophia sagax."

CHAPTER IV

ARCANUM ARCANORUM

THE great arcanum --- that is to say, the unutterable and inexplicable secret --- is the absolutewledge of good and of evil.
"When you have eaten the fruit of this tree, you will be as the gods," said the Serpent.

{Illustration on page 285 described:

"If you eat of it, you will die," replied Divine Wisdom.
Thus good and evil bear fruit on one same tree, and from one same root.
Good personified is God.
Evil personified is the Devil.
To know the secret or the formula of God is to be God.
To know the secret or the formula of the Devil is to be the Devil. {285}
To wish to be at the same time God and Devil is to absorb in one's self the most absolute antinomhe two most strained contrary forces; it is the wish to shut up in one's self an infinite antagons. It is to drink a poison which would extinguish the suns and consume the worlds.<<An allusion to Sa,the patron of adepts, who drank the poison generated by the churning of the 'Milk Ocean.' It is to put on the consuming robe of Deianira.
It is to devote one's self to the promptest and most terrible of all deaths.
Woe to him who wishes to know too much! For if excessive and rash knowledge does not kill him itl make him mad. {286}
To eat the fruit of the Tree of Knowledge of Good and Evil, is to associate evil with good, and ailate the one to the other.
It is to cover the radiant countenance of Osiris with the mask of Typhon.
It is to raise the sacred veil of Isis; it is to profane the sanctuary.

The rash man who dares to look at the sun without protection becomes blind, and from that moment him the sun is black.
We are forbidden to say more on this subject; we shall conclude our revelation by the figure of t pentacles.

This key is that of the Tarot. There are four suits, wands, caps,{sic} swords, coins or pentacleorresponding to the four cardinal points of Heaven, and the four living creatures or symbolic sigsadnumbers and letters formed in a circle; then the seven planetary signs, with the indication ofthirreetition signified by the three colours, to symbolize the natural world, the human world andthedivne orld, whose {288} hieroglyphic emblems compose the twenty-one trumps of our Tarot.
In the centre of the ring may be perceived the double triangle forming the Star or Seal of SolomoWEH NOTE: This is the classic mis-identification. By the aid of this key one can understand the universal symbolism of the ancient world, and note striking analogies with our dogmas. One will thus recognize that the divine revelation is permann nnature and humanity. One will feel that Christianity only brought light and heat into the unieralteple by causing to descend therein the spirit of charity, which is the Very Life of God Himslf.

EPILOGUE

Thanks be unto thee, O my God, that thou hast called me to this admirable light! Thou, the Suprentelligence and the Absolute Life of those numbers and those forces which obey thee in order to pol he infinite with inexhaustible creation! Mathematics proves thee, the harmonies of Nature prolam he, all forms as they pass by salute thee and adore thee!
Abraham knew thee, Hermes divined thee, Pythagoras calculated thee, Plato, in every dream of his us, aspired to {289} thee; but only one initiate, only one sage has revealed thee to the childreno ath, one alone could say of thee: "I and my Father are one." Glory then be his, since all his loy s hine!
Thou knowest, O my Father, that he who writes these lines has struggled much and suffered much; hs endured poverty, calumny, proscription, prison, the forsaking of those whom he loved: --- and ytnvr did he find himself unhappy, since truth and justice remained to him for consolation!
Thou alone art holy, O God of true hearts and upright souls, and thou knowest if ever I thought mf pure in thy sight! Like all men I have been the plaything of human passions. At last I conquee hm, or rather thou has conquered them in me; and thou hast given me for a rest the deep peace o tos wo have no goal and no ambition but Thyself.
I love humanity, because men, as far as they are not insensate, are never wicked but through erro through weakness. Their natural disposition is to love good, and it is through that love that to at given them as a support in all their trials that they must sooner or later be led back to th wrsipof justice by the love of truth.
Now let my books go where thy Providence shall send them! If they contain the words of thy wisdoey will be stronger than oblivion. If, on the contrary, they contain only errors, I know at leas htmy love of justice and of truth will survive them, and that thus immortality cannot fail to trasreth aspirations and wishes of my soul hat thou didst create immortal! {290}
